```
2044	## The Robustness Rework (2026-07-29)
2045	
2046	**This section is the rationale behind the phased plan in TODO.md.** TODO.md
2047	carries the step-by-step instructions; this section records why each phase is
2048	shaped the way it is, the design detail for the two new mechanisms (earned
2049	patches, distance-guided exploration), the corpus strategy, what was considered
2050	and deliberately not adopted, and the measurements preserved from the TODO
2051	items the plan retired. It synthesizes two parallel design sessions held
2052	2026-07-29 (one logged at \`agent-logs/2026-07-29_14-59_nothinking.md\`) plus the
2053	measured record above.
2054	
2055	### Goal and thesis
2056	
2057	The goal changed: **maximum robustness and minimum fragility over all plausible
2058	CAD-derived meshes**, not score on the current corpus. Fragility is
2059	operationalized as two headline numbers: the stage-2 tuned-constant count
2060	(34 at plan time; \`grep -h "^const \|^pub(crate) const \|^pub(super) const "
2061	src/stage2/*.rs | wc -l\`) and the **cliff count** — the number of constants
2062	whose ±2× perturbation collapses any model (four unrelated knobs once each
2063	flipped stepped_block medium from 83 surfaces to full per-facet collapse).
2064	
2065	The measured fragility concentrates in three structural debts, and the plan
2066	retires them in dependency order:
2067	
2068	1. **Evaluation is anecdotal.** 14 hand-watched models, Onshape/Parasolid-heavy;
2069	   three NURBS models rotted silently because nothing watched them. You cannot
2070	   claim generality you cannot measure → Phase 0 builds the instrument first.
2071	2. **Two of three tolerances are guesses.** \`--surface-tolerance\` defaults to
2072	   0.4 mm against a measured true sag of ~9e-4 mm (~400× too loose), making
2073	   centroid validation nearly vacuous; \`--angular-tolerance\` is a stand-in for
2074	   a discarded exporter setting. Both are measurable from the mesh itself
2075	   → Phases 0 (diagnostics) and 3 (regime flip).
2076	3. **One component — blind-patch seeding — forces most of the guard pile.**
2077	   \`seed_patch\`/\`condition_patch\` fit an unvalidated 32-face BFS neighbourhood;
2078	   the contamination lives on in the fit parameters, producing both the 323
2079	   NO-GROWTH seeds and plausible-but-wrong fits. Roughly two-thirds of stage
2080	   2's constants exist because tangent seams are invisible to it → Phases 1–2
2081	   replace the mechanism, Phase 4 deletes what that makes inert.
2082	
2083	Everything else measured principled — stage 1, sharp-edge regions, the DOF
2084	ladder, \`fit_validate\`, \`face_validates\`, flood, \`resolve_claims\`,
2085	relabel+refit, the locus machinery, stage 3 topological assembly, the
2086	\`--compare\` gates — **is deliberately not touched**.
2087	
2088	### Phase 1 design: earned patches (replaces the blind patch)
2089	
2090	Replace grab-then-repair with **grow-under-validation**: BFS from the seed,
2091	admitting a frontier face only if it validates against the current fit at
2092	vertex tolerance; when a face fails, attempt **promotion** up the ladder
2093	(plane → cylinder/cone or sphere → torus), refitting accepted+candidate and
2094	requiring *every already-accepted face* to still validate; if no type
2095	validates, the face is across a seam — exclude it and record it as a
2096	**frontier face** (Phase 2 consumes these). The patch can never straddle a
2097	junction by construction, which is the exact property the blind patch lacks.
2098	Commit semantics do not change: the earned (type, params, patch) goes to the
2099	unchanged \`grow_from\` flood/refit path, and every existing commit gate
2100	(\`MIN_AXIAL_SWEEP_DEG\`, torus ratio, \`MIN_*_FACES\`, \`resolve_claims\`) keeps
2101	its meaning.
2102	
2103	What makes promotion workable at tiny support — where a free least-squares fit
2104	is hopeless (sagitta arithmetic: over a 7.5° arc on r = 0.5 the sag is
2105	~0.001 mm against a 2e-5 mm quantum, so a free radius is ~2% ≈ 200× vtol
2106	wrong) — is that **facet normals are exact** and give closed-form,
2107	DOF-reducing initializers:
2108	
2109	- **Cylinder — axis = n₁ × n₂.** A planar facet spanning two rulings of a
2110	  cylinder has its normal exactly perpendicular to the axis, at any coarseness,
2111	  so two facets with non-parallel normals give the axis exactly on
2112	  strip/quad-tessellated surfaces. Radius+origin then come from
2113	  \`fit_circle_for_axis\` (cylindrical.rs), measured well-conditioned on short
2114	  arcs (r = 0.99998 / 0.99999 / 0.50008 from 65°/60°/40° arcs). **Provenance
2115	  caveat:** a general Delaunay triangle's normal is *not* exactly
2116	  perpendicular to the axis; measure axis error per provenance (Phase 0's OCCT
2117	  meshes), and degrade to the normal-covariance axis over the earned patch
2118	  where needed — still clean, still not blind.
2119	- **Sphere — concurrent normal lines.** For any planar facet inscribed in a
2120	  sphere, the line through the facet's **circumcenter** along its normal
2121	  passes exactly through the sphere centre (equidistance). Two facets → centre
2122	  at the near-intersection (common-perpendicular midpoint), radius = distance.
2123	- **Cone** — the existing normal-covariance axis (\`smallest_eigenvector_3x3\`)
2124	  over the earned patch, guarded by \`axis_normals_consistent\`, both existing.
2125	- **Torus — meridian coplanarity, the "open case" closed.** Every torus
2126	  normal line lies in the meridian plane through its point, which contains
2127	  the axis — so each facet contributes one coplanarity constraint
2128	  \`(pᵢ − a) · (nᵢ × d) = 0\` on the axis (point a, direction d; 4 DOF; the
2129	  system is bilinear — solve by alternation or pairwise elimination). Four
2130	  facets pin the axis; then (R, r) is a 2-D circle fit in (ρ, z) profile
2131	  coordinates — \`fit_circle_2d\` already exists (toroidal.rs). **Caveats:**
2132	  rank-deficient by construction on sphere data (any axis through the centre
2133	  satisfies it) and consistent-but-degenerate on cylinder data, so ladder
2134	  order and a conditioning floor must precede it; grid-aligned quads give
2135	  near-exact meridian normals, Delaunay triangles do not. **Time-boxed**: unit
2136	  test to ~1e-9 on synthetic tori before integrating; if conditioning does not
2137	  pan out, skip it — direct torus seeding stays as today and Phase 1 is not
2138	  blocked.
2139	
2140	Two behaviours need more than validation failure:
2141	
2142	- **Ambiguity → branch, don't choose.** Two same-height faces of a cylinder
2143	  validate as a sphere centred on the axis (the hole→sphere trap at patch
2144	  scale). Grow both branches; the false one should die within a face or two
2145	  (the next face at a different height is off the sphere by ≈ Δ²/2R ≫ vtol).
2146	  Instrument branch lifetime; persistent branches mean validation is less
2147	  sharp than believed — stop and ask.
2148	- **Bent tube → drift-triggered promotion.** A bend is locally a valid
2149	  cylinder (sag of the bend over a 2 mm patch at R = 14 is ~3.6e-5 mm), so
2150	  admission never fails and promotion never triggers on residual. The signal
2151	  is **systematic fit drift**: along a straight tube successive refits are
2152	  stationary; along a bend the fitted axis precesses with a consistent sign as
2153	  support accumulates. Test drift as sign-consistency of successive axis
2154	  deltas, *not* magnitude (a magnitude bar would be a new tuned constant —
2155	  stop and ask if one seems needed). Success retires \`promote_bend\` and the
2156	  \`BEND_*\` constants; failure keeps them, and that is fine.
2157	
2158	**Deleted when this lands (after ablation evidence):** \`condition_patch\`
2159	entirely, \`seed_patch\`'s jet filtering, \`SEED_PATCH_CAP\`, \`MAX_PATCH_DEPTH\`,
2160	\`PATCH_RADII\`, \`PATCH_TRIM_ROUNDS\`, \`MIN_CONDITION_FACES\`, likely
2161	\`MAX_TORUS_STARTS\` (multiple flood starts exist because the fit patch was
2162	unreliable).
2163	
2164	### Reference labeling, and what it settled (Phase 1.0, 2026-07-30)
2165	
2166	\`src/reference.rs\` (\`--reference-report\`) labels every mesh face with the
2167	reference face it came from and scores the mesh against that ground truth.
2168	Step-by-step numbers are in TODO.md 1.0; what belongs here is why it exists,
2169	why it is nearly free, and the four results that change later phases.
2170	
2171	**It is a read-back, not a new computation.** Stage 1's \`--compare\` already runs
2172	\`BRepExtrema_DistShapeShape\` from every mesh face centroid to a compound of the
2173	reference's faces. That call already determines which reference face won and at
2174	what (u,v); \`min_distance_to_shape\` was returning only the distance and dropping
2175	both. \`SupportOnShape2\` hands the winner back, so ground-truth labeling of the
2176	entire mesh costs one accessor call per face that was already being computed.
2177	The alternative that was on the table — a standalone probe — would have cost far
2178	more than that and measured the wrong object: it reads raw STL triangles, where
2179	the pipeline's mesh has been repaired and quad-fused, which is why
2180	\`step_measure_tolerance\` needed a \`--quad\` flag bolted on. **The general lesson
2181	is that a measurement belongs where the computation already happens**, and the
2182	same investigation found the cost of ignoring it: \`step_measure_tolerance\` still
2183	measures against the reference *solid*, so it silently reports exactly 0 for any
2184	deviation over OCCT's hard-coded 1 µm classifier tolerance (\`aTolerance = 0.001\`
2185	in \`BRepExtrema_DistShapeShape\`'s \`SolidTreatment\`) and is correct below it —
2186	exact where the answer does not matter, zero where it does. That is the same
2187	"never measure a distance against a solid" defect fixed in the pipeline on
2188	2026-07-29, in the one place the fix did not reach. It also refines that entry:
2189	the old stage-1 centroid check was not vacuous on all convex geometry, only
2190	above 1 µm, which is exactly why correct models looked right and nobody noticed.
2191	
2192	**1. The closed-form axis error is \`perpendicularity error / |n₁ × n₂|\`.**
2193	Verified to 0.1% on \`pipe_elbow_10\` Parasolid medium: a perpendicularity max of
2194	0.0280° over a conditioning p50 of 0.0271 predicts 1.033°, and the measured
2195	error is 1.0342°, identical across all 348 pairs. One relation explains every
2196	cylinder outlier in the sweep, and it separates two causes that a raw error
2197	number confounds — normals that are not perpendicular, and perfect normals
2198	amplified by an ill-conditioned cross product.
2199	
2200	**2. The Delaunay caveat did not reproduce, and the reason is architectural.**
2201	The Phase 1 design warned that "a general Delaunay triangle's normal is not
2202	exactly perpendicular to the axis" and told 1.4 to degrade that provenance to a
2203	covariance axis. Measured, OCCT's re-meshes read perpendicularity p50 = p95 =
2204	**0.0000°** and axis error **0.0000°** at every density on five of six models.
2205	The reason is already recorded under "Mesh provenance and the seam assumption":
2206	OCCT's Delaunay is *constrained* by the pre-discretized face boundaries, so a
2207	cylinder's triangles still span two rulings and their normals are still exactly
2208	perpendicular. The provenance fallback is not needed. The single genuine
2209	non-perpendicular case corpus-wide is \`l_bracket_simple\` Fusion medium
2210	(perpendicularity p50 0.2740°), a small hole carrying 96 facets over 75 mm².
2211	
2212	**3. Adjacent facets are the worst possible pairing, and finer meshes are
2213	worse.** \`|n₁ × n₂|\` for an adjacent pair is \`sin(angular step)\` — measured p50
2214	0.0432 on stepped_block fine, which is sin(2.48°) and independently recovers
2215	Onshape's 2.5° setting. So the amplification in (1) is ~23× on a fine mesh and
2216	grows as the mesh refines. This inverts the natural assumption that a denser
2217	mesh gives a better closed-form fit, and it is a design constraint on Phase 1.2
2218	rather than a property of any exporter: **pair the seed with the angularly most
2219	distant admitted facet**, where the factor is 1 and the axis error falls to the
2220	perpendicularity error itself (under 0.032° everywhere in the corpus). A 90°
2221	reading, which is the max on three rows, is the fully degenerate pair of two
2222	facets on one ruling strip — no information rather than a wrong answer.
2223	
2224	**4. The torus initializer should not be skipped.** Phase 1.1 time-boxes it on
2225	the suspicion that the meridian-coplanarity system is too ill-conditioned to
2226	solve. Its residual — the quantity the solve drives to zero — measures p50
2227	**2.354e-5 mm** over 23 863 torus facets on stepped_block fine, which is the
2228	mesh's own float32 quantum. The constraint is satisfied to the limit of what the
2229	mesh can express.
2230	
2231	**The seam assumption now has a direct measurement, and it holds.** Phase 0.5b
2232	measured only half of Design Principle #2 — that mesh *vertices* lie on the
2233	reference surfaces. The other half, that a *facet* never straddles a face
2234	boundary, is what the earned patch depends on and had never been measured.
2235	Labeling gives it for free: 51 of 157 483 facets across 79 meshes disagree about
2236	their own label across their own interior, **0.032%**. The number is an estimate
2237	rather than a bound in either direction — it over-reports where a neighbouring
2238	face curves back within reach of a sample point, and under-reports a straddle
2239	confined to a facet's rim, since the samples cover the middle of a facet — but
2240	both error terms are bounded by something this small, so the conclusion survives
2241	the imprecision even though the count does not. The only recurring cluster is
2242	exactly 12 facets on \`stepped_block_toroidal\` under OCCT coarse/medium and
2243	Fusion medium/fine, absent under Parasolid and under OCCT fine; consistent with
2244	coarse facets spanning that model's known narrow flat regions, and not chased.
2245	
2246	**Segmentation scoring closes the over-segmentation hole**, which this document
2247	recorded as needing its own step. Two area-weighted, threshold-free numbers from
2248	the same labels: \`ref coverage\` = \`Σ_R max_S area(R,S) / Σ area\` is 1.0 when no
2249	reference face was split across several of our surfaces, and \`surf purity\` =
2250	\`Σ_S max_R area(S,R) / Σ area\` is 1.0 when none of our surfaces spans several
2251	reference faces. They separate the two failure directions that "over-segmented"
2252	was being used for loosely. On the case that motivated it — \`rand_filleted_box_0\`,
2253	9 surfaces against a reference 26, passing every existing gate at 6.7e-3
2254	relative volume — they read **1.0000 and 0.2201**, correctly diagnosing merging
2255	rather than splitting, where a correct model reads 1.0000/1.0000. This satisfies
2256	the standing rule about metrics: asked what it returns for a grossly wrong
2257	input, it returns something unmistakably different from a right one.
2258	
2259	**Freeform reference faces, and the property that actually governs facet-normal
2260	exactness.** The normal report initially covered only analytic reference faces,
2261	which is 32–66% of a NURBS model's area — the rest was announced as unmeasured
2262	by the \`analytic area\` figure but not scored. Extending it needed only the
2263	footpoint (u,v) that \`ParOnFaceS2\` already reports from the same distance query,
2264	plus \`GeomLProp_SLProps\` (which arrived in the bindings on 2026-07-30).
2265	\`SLProps\` is used rather than a raw \`D1U × D1V\` because it answers
2266	\`IsNormalDefined\`, and a B-spline face has poles and seams where the normal
2267	genuinely does not exist; those facets are excluded rather than scored as an
2268	error of some size. Over the 8-model NURBS corpus every freeform facet evaluated
2269	and none landed on a pole.
2270	
2271	The result is not the obvious one. Freeform facet normals are 2–3 orders of
2272	magnitude less exact than analytic ones — \`bspline_terrain\` p50 0.9396°,
2273	\`hill_with_hole\` 0.9014°, against 0.0000° for planes — but that comparison is
2274	confounded, because the corpus's freeform models are ~100× coarser per facet.
2275	Ruledness separates them better: **\`bspline_wave\`'s *extrusion* faces read p50
2276	0.0507° at 16.7 mm² per facet, while \`bspline_terrain\`'s B-spline faces read
2277	0.9396° at 0.53 mm² per facet — the 30× coarser surface is 19× more exact.**
2278	
2279	**But ruledness is necessary, not sufficient — the real condition is that the
2280	tessellation be ruling-ALIGNED.** (Raised by the user, then measured; an earlier
2281	version of this section claimed ruledness governs, which is wrong.) The exact
2282	statement: a facet on a cylinder has its normal perpendicular to the axis **iff
2283	two of its vertices share a ruling**. Parametrize the cylinder and project the
2284	vertices along the axis; they land on a circle, the facet's plane is parallel to
2285	the axis iff those projections are collinear, and three points on a circle are
2286	collinear only when two coincide. A tessellator that lays triangles *across* the
2287	ruling family therefore produces skewed normals on a geometrically perfect
2288	cylinder.
2289	
2290	Measured over the 12 cylinder-bearing models, 12 275 cylinder facets: facets
2291	with an edge on a ruling are perpendicular to a worst case of **0.0006°**, while
2292	the **25.6%** that straddle three or more rulings reach **1.0528°**. Every
2293	non-zero perpendicularity in the corpus comes from a straddling facet, so
2294	alignment is the whole story — though straddling is necessary rather than
2295	sufficient for a *large* error, since the tilt grows with a facet's angular
2296	spread and shrinks with its axial one. That also explains the extrusion number
2297	above: 0.0507° rather than 0.0000° is itself evidence that those facets are not
2298	aligned, because alignment would force exactness.
2299	
2300	Where alignment comes from: quad fusion is a **23× discriminator but not a
2301	guarantee** — 2.7% of fused quads straddle against 62.3% of triangles (a
2302	coplanar quad on a cylinder *usually* spans two rulings, but four points on an
2303	elliptical section are coplanar too). Simple geometry gets alignment for free —
2304	a plain cylinder under OCCT is 120 quads / 0 triangles, 100% aligned, which is
2305	the real reason result (2)'s OCCT re-meshes read 0.0000°, sharper than "the
2306	rulings survive as face boundaries". Straddling concentrates where a tessellator
2307	cannot lay a clean strip: \`stepped_block_toroidal\` straddles 49–53% at every
2308	OCCT density, and its narrow flat regions between fillets are exactly its known
2309	pathology.
2310	
2311	This is *better* news for Phase 1.2 than the aggregate suggested, because the
2312	test needs no ground truth. Given a candidate axis, the minimum angular gap
2313	between a facet's vertices says whether that facet's normal can be trusted, so
2314	the earn loop can **select** the facets it initializes from rather than hoping.
2315	With the amplification law, the rule becomes: pair two **ruling-aligned** facets
2316	that are **angularly distant**. For Phase 5's freeform work the consequence is
2317	unchanged — facet normals cannot be assumed reliable on a doubly-curved patch at
2318	any density, since there are no rulings to align to.
2319	
2320	**A numerical fact worth not rediscovering.** An angle assertion near 0° cannot
2321	be tightened past about 1e-6°, because it goes through \`acos\`, which near an
2322	argument of 1 turns a 1e-16 rounding error into ~1.5e-8 rad. Assert on
2323	\`|â × b̂|\` instead, which is linear in the error and resolves to machine epsilon.
2324	Measured, not theorized: an exactly-constructed strip cylinder failed a 1e-9°
2325	perpendicularity assertion at one sample out of sixteen, and the same property
2326	passes at 1e-15 in cross-product form. The reported angles in \`reference.rs\` are
2327	printed to four decimals of a degree, far above that floor, so the report is
2328	unaffected — but do not read exactness below ~1e-6° out of a number from \`acos\`.
2329	
2330	### Phase 1.1 as built: what the closed-form initializers actually needed
2331	
2332	**Status 2026-08-06: the torus initializer is now wired in** — as a second
2333	candidate inside \`ToroidalSurface::fit_surface\`, LM-refined and selected against
2334	the geometric fit on residual. The other three remain unconsumed. See "The torus
2335	fitter's percentile, and what wiring the closed form in cost" below.
2336	
2337	All four initializers are exact on synthetic data (numbers in TODO.md 1.1), but
2338	three of the four constructions the Phase 1 design named turned out to be wrong,
2339	and the reasons generalize past this step.
2340	
2341	**The bilinear system was an artefact of the coordinates.** The design records
2342	the torus axis constraint \`(pᵢ − a)·(nᵢ × d) = 0\` as bilinear in the axis point
2343	and direction, to be solved "by alternation or pairwise elimination". Writing the
```
</details>

```
2344	axis as a *line* rather than a point-plus-direction removes the bilinearity
2345	outright: with the moment \`m = a × d\`, the constraint becomes \`qᵢ·d + nᵢ·m = 0\`
2346	where \`qᵢ = pᵢ × nᵢ\` — one linear equation per facet in six unknowns, solved as a
2347	null space with no iteration and no starting guess. The identity underneath is
2348	"every normal line of a surface of revolution meets the axis". The one caveat is
2349	scaling: the \`q\` block carries length units and the \`n\` block does not, so the
2350	system must be normalized to the patch's own centroid and extent first or the
2351	null space is lost in the scale mismatch rather than in the data.
2352	
2353	**A cone is not a surface of revolution as far as this solve is concerned.** The
2354	same normal-line construction is *rank-deficient on a single ring of cone
2355	facets*, and a single ring is exactly what a real cone tessellation is: a cone is
2356	ruled, so one row of quads spans its entire height, every facet centroid sits at
2357	the same axial position, and their normal lines then all meet the axis at the
2358	same point. Any line through that point satisfies the system. On a torus the
2359	meeting point slides with the tube angle, which is why the identical solve is
2360	well posed there. The lesson is that "surface of revolution" is not the property
2361	that makes the solve work — *variation in where the normal lines cross the axis*
2362	is.
2363	
2364	**The normal-covariance cone axis has a regime the plan did not account for.**
2365	A cone's normals lie on a cone of the same half-angle about the axis, so over a
2366	full revolution the covariance has eigenvalues \`cos²α/2\` (twice) and \`sin²α\`
2367	(once), and the axis is the smallest eigenvector only while **α < 35.26°**. That
2368	is not exotic: a 90° countersink is 45°, and the corpus's own \`cone_15x20\` is
2369	36.87°. Over the partial arc an earned patch actually has, the perpendicular
2370	spread is not isotropic either and it degrades before that. What replaces it is
2371	both exact and arc-free: **every facet plane of a cone contains the apex**,
2372	because a tangent plane along a ruling contains that ruling and all rulings meet
2373	at the apex — and this holds for the chord plane of a strip quad too, since two
2374	rulings still meet there. So \`nᵢ·A = nᵢ·pᵢ\` pins the apex from three independent
2375	normals, whatever the tessellation, and direction and half-angle follow from the
2376	vertex directions about it.
2377	
2378	**Facet normals are exact for directions and biased for angles.** The
2379	perpendicularity result from Phase 1.0 says a ruling-aligned facet normal is
2380	*exactly* perpendicular to the axis. It does not say the normal equals the
2381	tangent-plane normal: a strip quad's chord plane leans off it by
2382	\`n·d = sin α·cos(Δθ/2)/√(1 − sin²α sin²(Δθ/2))\`, an \`O(Δθ²/8)\` bias. Predicted
2383	1.5e-3 relative at Δθ = 0.11 rad, and the half-angle measured 9.985° against a
2384	true 10.000° — the same 1.5e-3. Vertices carry no such bias because they lie on
2385	the surface exactly, so wherever a quantity can be taken from vertices rather
2386	than from normals, it should be.
2387	
2388	**The conditioning floor is not a model selector, and the attempt to make it one
2389	was the familiar trap.** The meridian system is rank-deficient by construction on
2390	sphere data, so it was tempting to threshold the spectrum tightly enough to
2391	reject a sphere. Measured, the separation is not available: the degenerate sphere
2392	patch reads \`l1/l5\` = 2.06e-6 and the hardest genuine torus (R = 4, r = 3) reads
2393	9.2e-4, a factor of 450 with nothing but data-dependence between them — TODO
2394	rule 5's "detector needs just one threshold" reached from yet another direction.
2395	The floor stayed purely numerical, and *structure* does the discrimination for
2396	free: a sphere's (ρ, z) profile is a circle centred on the axis, so its fitted
2397	major radius comes out ~0 and the \`minor < major\` check rejects it outright. This
2398	is what the design meant by "ladder order and a conditioning floor must precede
2399	it" — type discrimination belongs to the ladder and to validation, never to a
2400	conditioning guard.
2401	
2402	### The torus fitter's percentile, and what wiring the closed form in cost (2026-08-06)
2403	
2404	Two changes to \`toroidal.rs\`, found by asking \`--viz-seeds\` why a vertex star of a
2405	fillet fails its own fit. Both are live.
2406	
2407	**1. The 25th percentile was the defect.** \`fit_torus_inner\` estimates the minor
2408	radius from normal-line intersections of adjacent facet pairs, which mix two
2409	populations — tube curvature (≈ r) and azimuthal curvature (≈ R ± r) — and it
2410	separated them by taking the 25th percentile, on the correct reasoning that tube
2411	readings are the smaller ones. On a large patch that works. On a 4–9 facet vertex
2412	star there are only a handful of pairs, so if one is a tube pair the percentile is
2413	an *azimuthal* reading, and every later step compounds it: the tube centres are
2414	placed at \`v + r·n\` with the wrong r, so they lie on a circle in the wrong plane,
2415	\`fit_circle_3d\` fits that circle, and LM refines into a basin whose axis is a
2416	quarter-turn off with the data sitting on the fitted torus's **inner equator** —
2417	the fitted \`R − r\` comes out equal to the true minor radius. Measured on
2418	\`filleted_hole_block\`'s r = 2 fillet: R = 20.7, r = 18.7, axis 89.86° off,
2419	residual 193× vertex tolerance, against the reference's own parameters fitting the
2420	same 16 vertices to **2.1 float32 quanta**. The sum-squared residual at the truth
2421	was **1.4e7× smaller**, so the global minimum was never in doubt — only the
2422	starting point. Across 215 rejected torus fits on four fillet models, 39% land
2423	within 5° of perpendicular and 30% have \`R − r\` within 15% of the true minor
2424	radius, while the fitted R itself ranges 0.03–66 mm.
2425	
2426	The fix is not a better percentile — that would be another statistic on a sample
2427	of four. It is to stop guessing which population an estimate came from and try
2428	several, keeping whichever produces the lowest-residual fit: the same
2429	select-the-best-candidate structure \`fit_torus\` already uses one level up for its
2430	inner/SOR/rescue methods. No tolerance is introduced; \`CANDIDATE_R_MAX\` only
2431	bounds work.
2432	
2433	**2. Phase 1.1's closed form as a second candidate — a large seed-level win and a
2434	clear corpus loss.** The two methods fail in different places rather than one
2435	dominating: the tube-centre method needs adjacent pairs that read the *tube*
2436	curvature and a small star may contain none, while the closed form reads the axis
2437	from the meridian-coplanarity null space and needs no such pair. On the seed above
2438	it returns R = 8.9996 / r = 1.9992 with the axis 0.049° off. Together the two
2439	changes take the fraction of torus facets with a workable seed set from
2440	25/54/90% to **100/100/100%** on \`filleted_hole_block\`, \`filleted_cylinder\` and
2441	\`filleted_pipe\`.
2442	
2443	What it costs, on \`dump_regions surfaces\`:
2444	
2445	| variant | stepped_block fine | medium |
2446	|---|---|---|
2447	| neither | 18/38/0/0/18/1 (75) | 33/31/0/0/19/1 (84) |
2448	| candidate-r sweep only | 20/38/0/0/18/0 (76) | 33/31/0/0/19/1 (84) |
2449	| both (current) | 72/37/0/0/18/0 (127) | 30/31/0/0/18/1 (80) |
2450	
2451	**The lesson is the one worth carrying forward.** Selection by residual is
2452	supposed to make a strictly-better candidate pool safe, and it is not: on a large
2453	support the closed form can win the residual comparison while being the worse
2454	surface for the *flood that follows*, and the plane count then explodes.
2455	**Residual on the current support does not predict how a fit will grow.** That is
2456	a property of the grow-and-compete design, not of the fit — which is why this
2457	landed anyway during the invariants rework, with \`remesh_cone_tangent_fine\`
2458	failing. That model is the canary this document already nominated for exactly this
2459	class of change.
2460	
2461	### Phase 1.2 as attempted: why the blind patch is load-bearing
2462	
2463	Phase 1.2 was to replace \`seed_patch\` + \`condition_patch\` with a patch grown
2464	under validation. It was implemented, measured over five rounds, and **not
2465	landed** (branch \`phase-1.2-earn-loop-attempt\`). Step-by-step numbers are in
2466	TODO.md 1.2. What belongs here is the shape of the result, because it revises
2467	the phase thesis rather than merely reporting a bug.
2468	
2469	**The thesis.** "Replace grab-then-repair with grow-under-validation … The patch
2470	can never straddle a junction by construction, which is the exact property the
2471	blind patch lacks." The closed-form initializers were what made this look
2472	possible: fit exactly from a handful of facets, so validation can gate growth
2473	from the first face.
2474	
2475	**What happened.** Each round removed one piece of the blind machinery and
2476	measured the consequence; each consequence was a mechanism the previous round
2477	had not predicted.
2478	
2479	1. *Validated-only growth starves.* A cone cannot be fitted from the two facets
2480	   a validated loop has when it must first decide, so it never grows. The blind
2481	   patch was solving a real chicken-and-egg: a fit needs support, and support
2482	   needs a fit.
2483	2. *Breadth-first collection is actively wrong on a developable.* A cone's
2484	   tangent plane is **constant along a ruling**, so a plane fitted to one facet
2485	   explains its neighbours up that ruling to ~5e-7 mm — well inside \`vtol\`. BFS
2486	   therefore walks the ruling, accepting coplanar facet after coplanar facet,
2487	   and produces a flat strip whose centred normal covariance is rank 0. No
2488	   curved surface can be initialized from it, ever. The loop was systematically
2489	   collecting the faces carrying no curvature information and rejecting the ones
2490	   that carried it.
2491	3. *Spread-first collection straddles junctions.* Fixing (2) by preferring the
2492	   candidate that most increases \`|n₁ × n₂|\` inverts the failure: "most
2493	   different normal" is a fair description of *a face on the neighbouring
2494	   surface*. Measured, it split every fillet of \`rounded_cube_offset_occt_medium\`
2495	   (12 cylinders → 24) and invented two spheres on
2496	   \`cone_tangent_planes_occt_medium\`. **Two different questions had been
2497	   conflated**: which faces to *collect* wants similarity, which faces to
2498	   *initialize from* wants spread. The blind patch had the first right and the
2499	   second wrong; the earn loop had the second right and the first backwards.
2500	4. *Curvature similarity is not sufficient either.* It is the right signal —
2501	   curvature distinguishes surfaces, normal direction does not — but on a
2502	   rounded cube the fillet radius **equals** the corner-sphere radius, so it
2503	   does not separate them.

2511	ladder, keep the connected component the fit explains, trimming toward the seed
2512	when the seed fails. That is \`seed_patch\` + \`condition_patch\` with a ladder
2513	choice added. **The design converged back onto what it set out to replace**, and
2514	the honest reading is that the blind patch and its conditioning are doing

2526	  0.93% at a 15.65° step, ~200× \`vtol\`. Only quantities read from *vertices*

2549	  (multi-source Dijkstra over the dual graph, **centroid-gap weights in
```

> 
> The kinematic family is the right choice: it's nested, includes torus via surface of revolution, leverages normals for discrimination, stays linear and incremental, and degeneracy maps directly to rank deficiency—exactly what "insufficient support" means. For tori specifically, the approach is axis-then-profile (treating torus as a circle swept in profile space), keeping the torus Levenberg-Marquardt as the exact refiner; quartic fitting gets rejected on degrees of freedom and conditioning grounds, though cyclide is noted as the principled but computationally heavy alternative.
> 
> Now checking the null-space dimensions claim for the 7-parameter system on a partial patch rather than a full revolution—like a small spherical cap...
> 
> For concurrent lines on a sphere, the null space is always 3D (rotations about center) regardless of patch extent. A small cylinder patch gives 2D null space, and a small torus patch gives 1D (rotation about the axis). But conditioning matters: how well the non-null directions get excited depends on patch size—this is the 450× spectrum gap between sphere and torus measurements. With just 2 facets on a cylinder, the rank of the stacked constraint rows is 2, leaving a 5D null space that contains the cylinder's true 2D plus spurious 3D directions. The enumerate-in-ladder-order validation approach handles this by checking whether a candidate surface (like a plane) actually validates all vertices across both facets at the tolerance threshold.
> 
> For a sphere, the kinematic requirement is that all normal lines must be concurrent. Two cylinder facet normals generically don't meet in 3D—they're skew lines. But if both facets are at the same height (adjacent rings), their normals both cross the cylinder axis at that same point, making them concurrent and allowing a sphere candidate to emerge. Vertex validation then confirms whether this sphere actually fits all 6 vertices within tolerance, which matches what we're measuring in practice.
>

```
Based on the search results, here's information about the Besl and Jain segmentation research:

"Segmentation through Variable-Order Surface Fitting" is a paper by Paul J. Besl and Ramesh C. Jain published in IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence in 1988.

**Overview:**
The work uses a piecewise-smooth surface model for image data that possesses surface coherence properties to develop an algorithm that simultaneously segments a large class of images into regions of arbitrary shape and approximates image data with bivariate functions.

**Two-Stage Approach:**
Besl and Jain introduced a two-step initial algorithm: (1) coarse segmentation, in which seed pixels are selected based on the mean and Gaussian curvature of each point and its sign; and (2) region growing, in which interactive region growing is used to refine the result based on a variable order bivariate surface fitting.

**Methodology:**
Surface curvature sign labeling provides an initial coarse image segmentation, which is refined by an iterative region-growing method based on variable-order surface fitting.

This seminal work has been influential in image segmentation and continues to be cited in contemporary research on point cloud segmentation and surface analysis.
```

```
Based on the search results, here's what I found about Bénière's work on reverse engineering CAD meshes:

## Overview

Bénière's work presents a comprehensive method to reconstruct a B-Rep model composed of planes, spheres, cylinders and cones from a 3D mesh whose vertex coordinates are considered exact.

## Method and Approach

The detection method uses curvature features to recover each primitive type, with segmentation based on the curvature feature computed for each vertex. Simple primitives like planes, spheres, cones and cylinders have specific curvature characteristics: planes have zero curvature at each point, spheres have equal curvature at all points, while cone and cylinder points are defined by one principal curvature equal to 0 with the other principal curvature equal to the inverse of the radius.

## Process Steps

The approach comprises three sequential steps: primitive extraction, wire construction, and solid creation. Initially, the matching geometric primitive (such as a plane, sphere, cylinder, or cone) corresponding locally to the 3D mesh of interest is determined by analyzing differential geometry operators.

## Application Context

In reverse engineering, the aim is to reconstruct a continuous model of an object (a B-Rep model for example) from a discretized representation (as a 3D mesh).
```

```
# Summary: Type-Constrained Direct Fitting of Quadric Surfaces

## (1) Kinematic Surface Fitting as First Step

The authors employ kinematic fitting to establish an initial linear system for quadric surface recovery. The unknowns include parameters representing rigid motions (rotation, translation) and scaling/dilation operations. Each equation row is constructed from either point constraints or normal vector constraints at sampled surface locations.

For points, the constraint comes from the intersection conditions; for normals, gradient-based information provides directional constraints. The method accommodates several field types including rotations (SO(3)), translations (R³), central dilations (scalar scaling), and helical motions (screw parameters).

## (2) Solution Space Dimension to Quadric Type Mapping

The dimensionality of the solution space directly correlates with quadric classification. Lower-dimensional solution spaces indicate more constrained geometric types (spheres, cylinders), while higher-dimensional spaces correspond to general quadrics. This relationship allows the authors to infer surface type from fitting rank.

## (3) Constraining to Specific Quadric Types

For specialized types like circular cones and cylinders, the authors impose additional algebraic constraints beyond the basic kinematic framework. These constraints reduce the degrees of freedom to match the geometric requirements of each surface class, forcing the fitted solution toward these restricted families.

## (4) Degenerate Cases and Linear Limitations

The paper acknowledges that "no linear method exists for cones and cylinders from points alone." Degenerate cases arise when fitting data cannot uniquely determine surface parameters; the authors address this through constraint-based disambiguation rather than purely algebraic approaches.

## (5) Torus Fitting

The document does not substantially address torus fitting methodology within the presented framework.
```

Research is done. Here's the analysis.

---

## TL;DR

Your instinct — one nested fitter, promote only when the data forces it, default to the simplest surface that fits — is sound and has a 40-year literature trail (Besl & Jain 1988 onward). But the **general algebraic quadric is the wrong nested family** for this codebase, for reasons your own measurements already pin down. The right family is hiding in plain sight: the **generalized kinematic system** — one linear solve from facet positions+normals whose *null-space dimension* is exactly the "simplest surface that fits" ladder, and whose four corners you have already built separately in `initialize.rs` without naming the whole. Tori then need **neither a quartic nor only a torus-specific fitter**: the kinematic solve pins the axis, and in (ρ,z) profile coordinates the torus is just a circle — a linear 2-DOF fit, with sphere/cylinder/cone as the circle's own degenerate cases.

A crucial framing first: this proposal is Phase 1.2's earn loop with the missing piece supplied. The earn loop died on a chicken-and-egg — "a cone cannot be fitted from the two facets a validated loop has when it must first decide, so it never grows." A rank-adaptive fitter dissolves exactly that: **the type is an output of the fit, never a precondition for it**. Whether that actually removes the need for the blind patch is a hypothesis to measure, not a fact — but it targets the specific mechanism that forced Phase 1.2 back onto `seed_patch`+`condition_patch`.

## Option A — the generalized kinematic ladder (recommended backbone)

Fit an instantaneous similarity velocity field `v(p) = γp + c×p + c̄` (7 unknowns: scaling γ, rotation c, translation c̄) tangent to the surface. Each facet contributes one linear row:

```
γ·(pᵢ·nᵢ) + c·(pᵢ×nᵢ) + c̄·nᵢ = 0
```

This is Pottmann's kinematic surface fitting as generalized by [Andrews & Séquin](http://graphics.berkeley.edu/papers/Andrews-GBI-2013-03/index.html) ([type-constrained companion paper](https://www.cad-journal.net/files/vol_11/CAD_11(1)_2014_107-119.pdf)) — both already in SURFACE_FITTING.md's sources. What the papers don't emphasize, and what makes it the answer to your question, is the **null-space dimension ladder**. For exact data:

| surface | tangent fields | null dim |
|---|---|---|
| plane | 2 in-plane translations, rotation ⊥, scaling about an in-plane point | **4** |
| sphere | rotations about center | **3** |
| cylinder | rotation about + translation along axis | **2** |
| cone | rotation about axis + dilation from apex (γ≠0) | **2** |
| torus / SOR / extrusion / helix | one field | **1** |
| freeform | none | **0** |

The dimension order *is* your simplicity order, and "insufficient support to pin down the result" becomes a *rank statement instead of a residual statement*: one facet gives rank 1 (everything fits → plane), and as faces accrue the null space contracts until the surviving generators name the type. Your sphere example resolves itself — plane at one facet, sphere as soon as three facet-normal lines are concurrent in 2D spread — and the cone never appears as an intermediate because sphere data never produces γ-structure. **The promotion chain is emergent; you never enumerate plane→cylinder→cone→sphere transitions.**

Three facts make this nearly free to try:

1. **You already built all four corners.** The torus meridian solve `qᵢ·d + nᵢ·m = 0` is the rigid sub-block (γ=0); cone Option A `γ(p·n) + a·n = 0` is the scaling sub-block (c=0); `cylinder()`'s `n₁×n₂` and `sphere()`'s concurrent normal lines are 2-row closed forms of the same structure. The unification is a 7×7 Gram matrix that *reads which structure is present* instead of assuming one per solve.
2. **It's incremental.** One rank-1 update of a 7×7 symmetric matrix per admitted face, one tiny eigensolve per promotion check. Your per-type LM fitters can't do that; this can, so the check can run at every face admission.
3. **Your measured lessons transfer verbatim**: normalize rows to patch centroid/extent (the meridian solve's recorded scale-mismatch lesson); take the result as a *seed* for the exact vertex-based fitters, never as the answer (chord-plane lean, O(Δθ²/8) ≈ 200× vtol); select initializer rows by ruling alignment (`min_ruling_gap_rad` exists) while collection stays validation-gated — which is precisely the similarity-vs-spread separation Phase 1.2 discovered the hard way.

One warning from your own record: **don't threshold the spectrum to decide rank** — that's the "detector needs just one threshold" trap, and the measured sphere-vs-torus conditioning gap (2.06e-6 vs 9.2e-4, only 450×, data-dependent) says the separation isn't there. Use the spectrum to *order* candidate interpretations; let vertex-tolerance validation decide, exactly as the jet prior works today.

And one honest limit: the normals channel kills the measured sphere-impostor pathology (393/397 cylinders) only when the support spans ≥2 distinct facet-centroid heights. A single-strip band — vertices on two rings, all centroids at one height — is genuinely sphere-ambiguous in *both* the vertex and normal views. There the load stays on structural gates (`MIN_AXIAL_SWEEP_DEG`, solid-angle coverage) and on Phase 1's branch-don't-choose. No fitting math dissolves a true ambiguity.

## Option B — the literal general quadric (your stated option)

Fit `x^TAx + b^Tx + c = 0` (Taubin/Pratt), watch A's eigenvalues for degeneracy, project onto the nearest constrained type. Assessment against your own measurements:

- **No torus** — the family omits the one surface every fillet needs, while *including* ellipsoids, paraboloids, hyperboloids that CAD essentially never uses. On support that barely pins a circular cylinder, the best algebraic quadric is generically some elliptic cylinder or hyperboloid — the impostor problem, made systematic.
- Your Invariants section measured that vertex-only algebra *cannot* discriminate types (impostor residuals at 0.2–0.5 quanta, sphere beating cylinder on its own support). A points-only quadric fitter inherits that blindness in full; the kinematic rows add the normal information that (partially) escapes it.
- The type-projection step is not sound: a circular cone needs a repeated eigenvalue, a condition [Andrews & Séquin](http://graphics.berkeley.edu/papers/Andrews-TCD-2013-06/index.html) note can't be imposed linearly or quadratically — this is already recorded as Option B's weakness in SURFACE_FITTING.md, with its ≥9-point cost and algebraic-distance bias.

Verdict: fine as a *triage* signal (analytic-vs-freeform, initializer of last resort), wrong as the backbone. "Upgrade to quartic" for tori is strictly worse: 34 DOF, ≥34 points, a degree-8 moment matrix whose conditioning is hopeless on patches, and an even larger zoo of irrelevant surfaces. Nobody in the reverse-engineering literature fits tori that way.

## Option C — per-type fitters with degeneracy-safe parameterizations

The conservative version: keep the exact per-type fitters and adopt the [Lukács–Martin–Marshall](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/BFb0055697) (ECCV 1998) parameterizations, which were designed for exactly your property: *"as the principal curvatures decrease, the results naturally become closer to the surfaces of simpler type, unlike other methods which may diverge."* Curvature-space parameters (k = 1/r, taper = tan α) put the simpler surface at a regular interior point of the parameter space instead of at infinity — the move your cone taper parameterization and the bent-tube k = 1/R already made independently. Promotion machinery is then the only new code, and the 2026-08-06 torus fix (candidate pool + select-best + validate) already established the house pattern for it. This is the smallest step, but it leaves the four hand-built initializers un-unified and keeps promotion as hand-enumerated edges.

## Option D — Dupin cyclides (the exotic one, for completeness)

The mathematically "right" single family containing exactly {plane, sphere, cylinder, cone, torus} plus ring cyclides is the [Dupin cyclides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dupin_cyclide): in the space of oriented spheres a cyclide's generating family is a conic, so there is a genuinely unified linear-ish fitting theory ([Druoton/Garnier/Langevin line of work](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00006-014-0453-5)). But that literature is construction/blend-oriented; robust fitting from meshes is thin; the Minkowski–Lorentz machinery is heavy; and the extra coverage (general cyclides) is a liability in a pipeline whose discipline is "simplest surface that validates." Not worth it now — worth remembering if variable-geometry blends ever become a target.

## How the torus question resolves

Under Option A the torus needs no special family at all: the 1-dim rotational null space hands you the axis, and in (ρ,z) profile coordinates the SOR family becomes 2D curve fitting — line ρ=R (cylinder), sloped line (cone), circle centered on axis (sphere), offset circle (torus) — all linear, all trivially nested, which is the "Unified SOR Framework" section SURFACE_FITTING.md already sketches. Keep the existing torus LM (and the tube-center method) as the vertex-based refiner; the measured meridian-solve residual (p50 at the float32 quantum) says the axis half already works.

## What the fitting math does *not* solve

Carrying over from the measured record, unchanged by any option: **plane starvation of the B-spline rung** (a single-facet plane always validates, so freeform regions would tile into analytic confetti — the NURBS-collapse measurement; the leftover-coherence policy must survive); **branching at genuine ambiguity** (ring pairs); **trim-not-filter** at promotion failure (excluded face → frontier, keep the patch); boundary placement stays with flood + `resolve_claims`; and single-seed growth makes seed choice matter, which is exactly what the S/G invariant families instrument.

## What I'd measure first

House style says instrument before mechanism, and all three probes fit the `--invariant-report` harness:

1. **Rank-ladder read on truth**: accumulate the normalized 7×7 on each reference face's own facets at increasing support; measure at what support, eigengap, and straddling fraction the null-dim reads the true type. (~150 lines, diagnostics only.)
2. **Junction contamination race**: same read on supports deliberately grown across tangent junctions — does the rank signal move before or after vertex validation fails?
3. **Strip-band census**: what fraction of corpus cylinders are single-centroid-height strips (where sphere stays live in both views) — this sizes the permanent role of the structural gates.

## Literature map

- [Besl & Jain 1988, *Segmentation through Variable-Order Surface Fitting*](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/3881/) — the direct precedent for seed-and-promote region growing (curvature-sign seeds, lowest order that fits, escalate on demand).
- [Lukács, Martin, Marshall, ECCV 1998](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/BFb0055697) (+ [journal version](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/2400512_Geometric_Least-Squares_Fitting_of_Spheres_Cylinders_Cones_and_Tori)) — graceful-degeneration parameterizations for sphere/cylinder/cone/torus.
- [Andrews & Séquin, *Generalized, Basis-Independent Kinematic Surface Fitting*](http://graphics.berkeley.edu/papers/Andrews-GBI-2013-03/index.html) and [*Type-Constrained Direct Fitting of Quadric Surfaces*](https://www.cad-journal.net/files/vol_11/CAD_11(1)_2014_107-119.pdf) — the Option A machinery.
- [Leonardis et al., recover-and-select](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/3-540-57956-7_35) — grow many, keep the simplest description; use its orchestration, not its MDL scoring (your 2026-07-28 measurement refuted complexity pricing in this regime).
- [Wu & Kobbelt 2005, hybrid VSA](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/j.1467-8659.2005.00852.x), [quadric-proxy VSA](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2016/12/Quadric-Surface-Extraction-by-Variational-Shape-Approximation.pdf), and [VSA + model selection](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/j.1467-8659.2009.01577.x) — the global-competition alternative already in your reserve list.
- [Bénière et al.](https://perso.liris.cnrs.fr/gilles.gesquiere/Publications/2011_SPIE_EI_Beniere.pdf) ([CAD 2013](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0010448513001012)) — the only sustained prior work in your exact regime (exact-vertex CAD meshes → B-rep); notably stops at plane/sphere/cylinder/cone, no torus.
- [Split-and-fit, SIGGRAPH 2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2406.05261) — representative of the current learned/partition line; doesn't meet the exactness/determinism bar but useful as a survey of the field's framing.

My recommendation: **Option A as the backbone, with Option C's parameterization discipline in the refiners**, quadrics demoted to triage, cyclides declined. If you want, I can record this survey in SURFACE_FITTING.md alongside the cone-options section (it's the same genre), or go straight to building probe 1.

```
2204	fn patch_size_probe(
2205	    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
2206	    faces: &[usize],
2207	    truth_axis: ([f64; 3], [f64; 3]),
2208	    is_cone: bool,
2209	    model: &str,
2210	    ref_idx: usize,
2211	    kind: &str,
2212	) {
2213	    const SIZES: [usize; 10] = [2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 16, 24, 32, 64];
2214	    const STARTS: usize = 8;
2215	    let member: HashSet<usize> = faces.iter().copied().collect();
2216	    let (_, want_dir) = truth_axis;
2217	    let want = unit(want_dir);
2218	    let ang = |d: [f64; 3]| -> f64 {
2219	        let d = unit(d);
2220	        let c = [
2221	            d[1] * want[2] - d[2] * want[1],
2222	            d[2] * want[0] - d[0] * want[2],
2223	            d[0] * want[1] - d[1] * want[0],
2224	        ];
2225	        norm(c).clamp(0.0, 1.0).asin().to_degrees()
2226	    };
2227	    let stride = (faces.len() / STARTS).max(1);
2228	    for &start in faces.iter().step_by(stride).take(STARTS) {
2229	        // One BFS from the start, recording the axis error each time the
2230	        // accumulated patch reaches the next size on the ladder.
2231	        let mut patch = vec![start];
2232	        let mut seen: HashSet<usize> = HashSet::from([start]);
2233	        let mut frontier = vec![start];
2234	        let mut next_size = 0usize;
2235	        while next_size < SIZES.len() {
2236	            while next_size < SIZES.len() && patch.len() >= SIZES[next_size] {
2237	                let target = SIZES[next_size];
2238	                let sub: Vec<usize> = patch[..target.min(patch.len())].to_vec();
2239	                let got = if is_cone {
2240	                    super::initialize::cone(&sub, mesh, 5e-5)
2241	                } else {
2242	                    super::initialize::cylinder(&sub, mesh)
2243	                };
2244	                if let Some(
2245	                    super::initialize::Initial::Cylinder { axis_dir, .. }
2246	                    | super::initialize::Initial::Cone { axis_dir, .. },
2247	                ) = got
2248	                {
2249	                    println!(
2250	                        "PATCHSIZE\t{model}\t{ref_idx}\t{kind}\t{target}\t{:.6}",
2251	                        ang(axis_dir)
2252	                    );
2253	                }
2254	                next_size += 1;
2255	            }
2256	            // Expand one ring.
2257	            let mut ring = Vec::new();
2258	            for &fi in &frontier {
2259	                let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
2260	                for &ni in &mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[..vc] {
2261	                    if ni < 0 {
2262	                        continue;
2263	                    }
2264	                    let nj = ni as usize;
2265	                    if member.contains(&nj) && seen.insert(nj) {
2266	                        patch.push(nj);
2267	                        ring.push(nj);
2268	                    }
2269	                }
2270	            }
2271	            if ring.is_empty() {
2272	                break;
2273	            }
2274	            frontier = ring;
2275	        }
2276	    }
2277	}
2278	
2279	/// Measure the section's cylinder/cone seed rule on one developable reference
2280	/// face: enumerate the chains it admits, test both readings of its coplanarity
2281	/// clause, and fit each chain's closed-form axis against ground truth.
2282	fn seed_rule_probe(
2283	    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
2284	    faces: &[usize],
2285	    truth_axis: ([f64; 3], [f64; 3]),
2286	    is_cone: bool,
2287	) -> SeedRuleStats {
2288	    let member: HashSet<usize> = faces.iter().copied().collect();
2289	    let mut st = SeedRuleStats {
2290	        pairs: 0,
2291	        six_verts: 0,
2292	        all_coplanar: 0,
2293	        four_coplanar: 0,
2294	        ax_err: Vec::new(),
2295	        ax_err_admitted: Vec::new(),
2296	        selected_err: f64::NAN,
2297	    };
2298	    let (_, want_dir) = truth_axis;
2299	    let want = unit(want_dir);
2300	    let ang = |d: [f64; 3]| -> f64 {
2301	        let d = unit(d);
2302	        let c = [
2303	            d[1] * want[2] - d[2] * want[1],
2304	            d[2] * want[0] - d[0] * want[2],
2305	            d[0] * want[1] - d[1] * want[0],
2306	        ];
2307	        // |sin| form: linear in the error near 0, unlike acos.
2308	        norm(c).clamp(0.0, 1.0).asin().to_degrees()
2309	    };
2310	
2311	    // The selection rule Phase 1.1 actually built: widest normal pair over the
2312	    // whole face, re-picked among ruling-aligned facets. The contrast between
2313	    // this and a bare chain is the point of the measurement.
2314	    st.selected_err = match if is_cone {
2315	        super::initialize::cone(faces, mesh, 5e-5)
2316	    } else {
2317	        super::initialize::cylinder(faces, mesh)
2318	    } {
2319	        Some(super::initialize::Initial::Cylinder { axis_dir, .. })
2320	        | Some(super::initialize::Initial::Cone { axis_dir, .. }) => ang(axis_dir),
2321	        _ => f64::NAN,
2322	    };
2323	
2324	    // "Coplanar" needs a scale-free bar. 1e-6 of the point set's own extent is
2325	    // five orders below a tessellation step and is a validity floor, not a
2326	    // tuning knob: a fused quad is coplanar to rounding, distinct rulings are
2327	    // separated by the exporter's angular setting.
2328	    let copl_rel = 1.0e-6;
2329	    let (chains, considered) = chains_with_six_vertices(mesh, faces, &member, MAX_SEED_PAIRS);
2330	    st.pairs = considered;
2331	    st.six_verts = chains.len();
2332	    for chain in &chains {
2333	        let vs = vertices_of(mesh, chain);
2334	        let pts: Vec<[f64; 3]> = vs.iter().map(|&v| vertex_pos(mesh, v)).collect();
2335	        let all_cop = planarity(&pts) <= copl_rel;
2336	        if all_cop {
2337	            st.all_coplanar += 1;
2338	        }
2339	        if any_four_coplanar(&pts, copl_rel) {
2340	            st.four_coplanar += 1;
2341	        }
2342	        let got = if is_cone {
2343	            super::initialize::cone(chain, mesh, 5e-5)
2344	        } else {
2345	            super::initialize::cylinder(chain, mesh)
2346	        };
2347	        if let Some(super::initialize::Initial::Cylinder { axis_dir, .. })
2348	        | Some(super::initialize::Initial::Cone { axis_dir, .. }) = got
2349	        {
2350	            let err = ang(axis_dir);
2351	            st.ax_err.push(err);
2352	            if !all_cop {
2353	                st.ax_err_admitted.push(err);
2354	            }
2355	        }
2356	    }
2357	    st
2358	}
2359	
2360	fn quantile(v: &mut [f64], q: f64) -> f64 {
2361	    if v.is_empty() {
2362	        return f64::NAN;
2363	    }
2364	    v.sort_by(|a, b| a.partial_cmp(b).unwrap());
2365	    let i = ((v.len() - 1) as f64 * q).round() as usize;
2366	    v[i]
2367	}

2382	}
```

```
2515	/// Jaccard index of two sorted face sets.
2516	fn jaccard(a: &[usize], b: &HashSet<usize>) -> f64 {
2517	    if a.is_empty() && b.is_empty() {
2518	        return 1.0;
2519	    }
2520	    let inter = a.iter().filter(|f| b.contains(f)).count();
2521	    let union = a.len() + b.len() - inter;
2522	    if union == 0 {
2523	        1.0
2524	    } else {
2525	        inter as f64 / union as f64
2526	    }
2527	}

2531	pub fn report(

2534	    model: &ReferenceModel,
2535	    labels: &[FaceLabel],
2536	    jet: &[JetField],

2553	        .map(|fi| face_centroid(&mesh.faces[fi], &mesh.vertices))
2554	        .collect();
2555	
2556	    // Group facets by reference face.
2557	    let mut by_ref: HashMap<usize, Vec<usize>> = HashMap::new();
2558	    let mut unlabeled = 0usize;
2559	    for (fi, lab) in labels.iter().enumerate() {
2560	        match lab.face() {
2561	            Some(r) => by_ref.entry(r).or_default().push(fi),
2562	            None => unlabeled += 1,
2563	        }
2564	    }
2565	    for v in by_ref.values_mut() {

2582	    // fit that spans the seam is RIGHT even though it does not equal any one

2594	    }
```

```
1	//! Closed-form initializers (Phase 1.1): a surface's parameters from a handful
2	//! of facets, exactly, with no iteration and no seed patch.
3	//!
4	//! Phase 1's earned patch grows face by face under validation, which means it
5	//! must fit a surface to *tiny* support — where a free least-squares fit is
6	//! hopeless. The sagitta arithmetic: over a 7.5° arc on r = 0.5 the sag is
7	//! ~0.001 mm against a 2e-5 mm mesh quantum, so a free radius comes out ~2%
8	//! wrong, about 200× the vertex tolerance. What rescues it is that **facet
9	//! normals carry exact information**, which turns each surface type into a
10	//! small linear problem rather than an optimization.
11	//!
12	//! Nothing here iterates and nothing here is tuned. Every constant is a
13	//! numeric-conditioning floor — it decides whether a quantity is *defined*,
14	//! never whether a fit is *good* — and each is documented as such at its
15	//! definition.
16	//!
17	//! # Which facets to initialize from, and why it matters more than the formula
18	//!
19	//! Phase 1.0 measured two things that make facet *selection* part of the
20	//! algorithm rather than an afterthought (numbers in TODO.md 1.0):
21	//!
22	//! 1. **The axis error is \`perpendicularity error / |n₁ × n₂|\`**, verified to
23	//!    0.1%. Adjacent facets differ in normal by the exporter's angular step, so
24	//!    \`|n₁ × n₂| ≈ sin(step)\` — 0.043 on a fine mesh, a ~23× amplification that
25	//!    gets *worse* as the mesh refines. So pair the angularly most distant
26	//!    facets available, never neighbours.
27	//! 2. **A facet's normal is perpendicular to the axis iff two of its vertices
28	//!    share a ruling** ([\`min_ruling_gap_rad\`]). 25.6% of corpus cylinder facets
29	//!    straddle three or more rulings, and they carry *all* of the
30	//!    perpendicularity error — aligned facets are exact to 0.0006° worst case
31	//!    over the whole corpus, straddling ones reach 1.0528°.
32	//!
33	//! Both are checkable here without ground truth, so [\`select_axis_pair\`] does
34	//! it in two passes: pick the most distant normal pair to get a provisional
35	//! axis, then re-pick among the facets that are ruling-aligned *with respect to
36	//! that axis*. The instrument that measured (2) and the code that acts on it
37	//! call the same function, so they cannot drift apart.
38	
39	// Phase 1.1 builds and pins these; Phase 1.2's earn loop is what calls them.
40	// Until then the constructors are reachable only from the tests, which is the
41	// point of unit-testing them first — the allow comes off when \`grow\` uses them.

47	/// A closed-form fit, ready to hand to validation and growth.
48	#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq)]
49	pub(crate) enum Initial {
50	    Cylinder {
51	        axis_pos: [f64; 3],
52	        axis_dir: [f64; 3],
53	        radius: f64,
54	    },
55	    Sphere {

57	        radius: f64,
58	    },

73	// Local vector helpers (stage 2 shares only cross3/dot3/normalize3).
74	fn add(a: [f64; 3], b: [f64; 3]) -> [f64; 3] {
75	    [a[0] + b[0], a[1] + b[1], a[2] + b[2]]
76	}
77	
78	fn sub(a: [f64; 3], b: [f64; 3]) -> [f64; 3] {
79	    [a[0] - b[0], a[1] - b[1], a[2] - b[2]]
80	}
81	
82	fn scale(a: [f64; 3], s: f64) -> [f64; 3] {
83	    [a[0] * s, a[1] * s, a[2] * s]
84	}
85	
86	fn norm(a: [f64; 3]) -> f64 {
87	    dot3(&a, &a).sqrt()
88	}

90	// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
91	// Conditioning floors
92	// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
93	
94	/// Below this, two unit normals are parallel to within double-precision noise
95	/// and their cross product carries no direction at all.
96	///
97	/// A validity floor, not a threshold: it decides whether the axis *exists*, not
98	/// whether it is good enough. The quality of a well-defined axis is governed by
99	/// the amplification law in the module docs, which is a continuous relation with
100	/// no cut in it.
101	const MIN_CROSS_NORM: f64 = 1.0e-9;
102	
103	/// Below this angular gap (radians) two of a facet's vertices lie on the same
104	/// ruling.
105	///
106	/// Not a tuned threshold: measured over the corpus the two cases are separated
107	/// by five orders of magnitude of empty space — vertices on one ruling differ
108	/// only by float32 mesh noise (p50 reads 5.551e-16 rad) while distinct rulings
109	/// are separated by the exporter's angular step (p95 1.178e-1 rad). Any cut
110	/// between gives the same answer.
111	pub(crate) const RULING_SAME_RAD: f64 = 1.0e-6;
112	
113	/// The second-smallest eigenvalue must exceed this fraction of the largest for
114	/// the null space to be computable at all.
115	///
116	/// **A numerical floor and nothing more.** It was tempting to make this a rank
117	/// test sharp enough to tell a torus from a sphere — the meridian system is
118	/// rank-deficient by construction on sphere data, where every line through the
119	/// centre satisfies it. Measured, that discrimination is not available at this
120	/// ratio: the degenerate sphere patch reads \`l1/l5\` = 2.06e-6 and the hardest
121	/// genuine torus (R = 4, r = 3) reads 9.2e-4, a factor of 450 with nothing but
122	/// data-dependence in between. Choosing a cut there is the "detector needs just
123	/// one threshold" trap, so it is not chosen here.
124	///
125	/// What separates the cases instead is *structure*, at no cost: a sphere's
126	/// (ρ, z) profile is a circle centred on the axis, so its fitted major radius
127	/// comes out ~0 and [\`torus\`]'s \`minor < major\` check rejects it outright. Type
128	/// discrimination is the ladder's job and validation's, not this floor's — which
129	/// is what the plan meant by "ladder order and a conditioning floor must precede
130	/// it".
131	const MIN_NULLSPACE_SEPARATION: f64 = 1.0e-12;
132	
133	// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
134	// Facet selection
135	// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
136	
137	/// The smallest angular gap, in radians, between any two of a facet's vertices
138	/// measured around the axis \`(axis_pos, axis_dir)\` — zero exactly when two
139	/// vertices share a ruling.
140	///
141	/// This decides whether a facet normal on a ruled surface is perpendicular to
142	/// the axis at all. Parametrize the cylinder as
143	/// \`p(θ, z) = a + r(cos θ · u + sin θ · v) + z · d\`. The facet's plane contains
144	/// \`d\` — equivalently \`n ⟂ d\` — iff the vertices, projected along \`d\` onto the
145	/// plane perpendicular to it, are collinear. They land on a circle of radius
146	/// \`r\`, and three points on a circle are collinear only when two of them
147	/// coincide. **So \`n ⟂ d\` exactly when two vertices share a ruling**, and a
148	/// triangle straddling three distinct rulings tilts its normal off perpendicular
149	/// however perfect the underlying cylinder is.
150	pub(crate) fn min_ruling_gap_rad(
151	    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
152	    fi: usize,
153	    axis_pos: [f64; 3],
154	    axis_dir: [f64; 3],
155	) -> Option<f64> {
156	    let mut d = axis_dir;
157	    if normalize3(&mut d) == 0.0 {
158	        return None;
159	    }
160	    let (u, v) = perpendicular_basis(&d);
161	
162	    let face = &mesh.faces[fi];
163	    let n = face.vertex_count as usize;
164	    if n < 3 {
165	        return None;
166	    }
167	    let mut thetas = Vec::with_capacity(n);
168	    for k in 0..n {
169	        let vtx = &mesh.vertices[face.vertex_indices[k]];
170	        let w = [
171	            vtx.x - axis_pos[0],
172	            vtx.y - axis_pos[1],
173	            vtx.z - axis_pos[2],
174	        ];
175	        let axial = dot3(&w, &d);
176	        let radial = [
177	            w[0] - axial * d[0],
178	            w[1] - axial * d[1],
179	            w[2] - axial * d[2],
180	        ];
181	        thetas.push(dot3(&radial, &v).atan2(dot3(&radial, &u)));
182	    }
183	
184	    let mut min_gap = f64::MAX;
185	    for i in 0..n {
186	        for j in (i + 1)..n {
187	            let mut delta = (thetas[i] - thetas[j]).abs();
188	            if delta > std::f64::consts::PI {
189	                delta = std::f64::consts::TAU - delta;
190	            }
191	            min_gap = min_gap.min(delta);
192	        }
193	    }
194	    Some(min_gap)
195	}
196	
197	/// \`|n_i × n_j|\` — how well-conditioned the pair is as an axis source, and
198	/// exactly the denominator in the amplification law.
199	fn pair_conditioning(a: &[f64; 3], b: &[f64; 3]) -> f64 {
200	    let c = cross3(a, b);
201	    (c[0] * c[0] + c[1] * c[1] + c[2] * c[2]).sqrt()
202	}
203	
204	/// The most angularly distant pair of facet normals in \`faces\`.
205	fn widest_normal_pair(faces: &[usize], mesh: &ConnectedMesh) -> Option<(usize, usize, f64)> {
206	    let mut best: Option<(usize, usize, f64)> = None;
207	    for (a, &fi) in faces.iter().enumerate() {
208	        let Some(ni) = mesh.faces[fi].normal else {
209	            continue;
210	        };
211	        for &fj in &faces[a + 1..] {
212	            let Some(nj) = mesh.faces[fj].normal else {
213	                continue;
214	            };
215	            let k = pair_conditioning(&ni, &nj);
216	            if best.is_none_or(|(_, _, bk)| k > bk) {
217	                best = Some((fi, fj, k));
218	            }
219	        }
220	    }
221	    best
222	}
223	
224	/// Pick the pair of facets to take an axis from: ruling-aligned, and as
225	/// angularly distant as the patch allows.
226	///
227	/// Two passes, because alignment is defined relative to an axis we do not have
228	/// yet. The first pass gives a provisional axis good enough to *classify*
229	/// facets — misjudging alignment needs an axis error of order the angular step,
230	/// far beyond what pass one produces — and the second pass re-picks among the
231	/// facets that survive. If too few survive, the provisional pair stands: a
232	/// worse-conditioned axis is still better than no axis, and validation
233	/// downstream is what decides whether the fit lives.
234	///
235	/// Returns the pair and the axis direction it implies.
236	fn select_axis_pair(faces: &[usize], mesh: &ConnectedMesh) -> Option<(usize, usize, [f64; 3])> {
237	    let axis_of = |fi: usize, fj: usize| -> Option<[f64; 3]> {
238	        let (ni, nj) = (mesh.faces[fi].normal?, mesh.faces[fj].normal?);
239	        let mut axis = cross3(&ni, &nj);
240	        if normalize3(&mut axis) < MIN_CROSS_NORM {
241	            return None;
242	        }
243	        Some(axis)
244	    };
245	
246	    let (pi, pj, _) = widest_normal_pair(faces, mesh)?;
247	    let provisional = axis_of(pi, pj)?;
248	
249	    // Any point on the provisional axis will do to measure ruling gaps: the
250	    // gap is an angle about the axis direction, and shifting the reference
251	    // point along or across the axis moves every vertex's θ together.
252	    let anchor = face_centroid(&mesh.faces[pi], &mesh.vertices);
253	    let aligned: Vec<usize> = faces
254	        .iter()
255	        .copied()
256	        .filter(|&fi| {
257	            min_ruling_gap_rad(mesh, fi, anchor, provisional)
258	                .is_some_and(|gap| gap < RULING_SAME_RAD)
259	        })
260	        .collect();
261	
262	    if aligned.len() >= 2 {
263	        if let Some((ai, aj, _)) = widest_normal_pair(&aligned, mesh) {
264	            if let Some(axis) = axis_of(ai, aj) {
265	                return Some((ai, aj, axis));
266	            }
267	        }
268	    }
269	    Some((pi, pj, provisional))
270	}
271	
272	/// Every distinct vertex index used by \`faces\`.
273	fn patch_vertices(faces: &[usize], mesh: &ConnectedMesh) -> Vec<usize> {
274	    let mut out = Vec::new();

276	        let f = &mesh.faces[fi];

279	            if !out.contains(&vi) {
280	                out.push(vi);

282	        }
283	    }
284	    out
285	}

299	    let verts = patch_vertices(faces, mesh);
```

```
878	pub(crate) fn torus(faces: &[usize], mesh: &ConnectedMesh) -> Option<Initial> {
879	    let (pos, dir) = axis_line_from_normals(faces, mesh)?;

881	    if profile.len() < 3 {

890	        pos[1] + dir[1] * z0,

892	    ];
893	    Some(Initial::Torus {
894	        centre,
895	        axis_dir: dir,
896	        major,
897	        minor,
898	    })
```

> I'm working through the kinematic field equations for the 7×7 system, extending the 6×6 case by adding a column for p·n. The tangency condition v(p)·n = 0 gives me γ(p·n) + c·(p×n) + c̄·n = 0, which simplifies using the cross product identity to express the constraint in terms of the generalized coordinates.
> 
> Now I'm checking how this system behaves under coordinate transformations — scaling positions by the patch extent and translating the origin. The rank of the system should be invariant under these affine changes, which matters for understanding the solution space of similarity velocity fields. I need to verify that the parameterization {γp + c×p + c̄} transforms consistently when I shift to local coordinates.
> 
> Starting to work through the constraint equations for a plane with normal n₀ passing through point p₀, where the tangent similarity fields must satisfy v·n = 0 everywhere on that plane. That checks out—the null space has dimension 4 as expected for a plane. The generators are a rotation around the normal, two independent in-plane translations, and a mixed mode combining the scaling parameter and the normal component. For a sphere centered at the origin, the constraints force both the scaling and translation normal components to zero, leaving only the three rotational degrees of freedom. Now looking at the cylinder case with axis through the origin. So the null space has dimension 2, corresponding to rotation about the axis and translation along it. For a cone with apex at the origin and axis d, the tangent planes all pass through the apex, making p·n = 0 for every tangent plane. This means γ is completely free—giving us the dilation generator from the apex. I'm checking whether rotation about the axis works with c = μd and c̄ = 0, using the fact that d·(p×n) relates to the tangent condition for the cone.
> 
> The key distinction between a cone and cylinder is that both have 2-dimensional tangent field spaces, but a cylinder's generators exclude any dilation component, while a cone's includes dilation about the apex as one of its generators. So the tangent vector for a cone with γ=1, c=0, c̄=0 points along the ruling from the apex, confirming it's tangent to the surface. I'm realizing the classification needs to be more careful: planes can have γ≠0 members (dilations about in-plane points), while spheres have γ=0 throughout. A cleaner way to organize this is by dimension plus whether γ can be nonzero and whether c can be nonzero, which gives a concrete test—for instance, dim 2 surfaces are cones specifically when some null vector has γ≠0.
> 
> Extending the classification: dim 1 surfaces split into toruses or surfaces of revolution if c≠0 with γ=0, extrusions if c=0, or spirals and conical helices if γ≠0 with rotation; dim 0 is freeform. I'm planning to build a probe that accumulates normalized 7×7 matrices on reference faces at increasing support levels, then measures at what support size, eigengap, and straddling fraction the null dimension correctly identifies the true surface type. For the implementation, I'm thinking of creating a new kinematic module in the stage2 directory to house the mathematical logic so it's reusable and testable, with a probe function in the invariants harness gated behind a feature flag. The probe should report the full eigenvalue spectrum normalized by the largest eigenvalue, then compute what dimensionality various thresholds would yield, and identify where the largest eigengap sits — this gives honest measurement without imposing a decision threshold. For the output structure, I'll record the model, reference index, true kind, support facet count, straddle fraction, all seven normalized eigenvalues in ascending order, and the position of the largest ratio gap between consecutive eigenvalues.
> 
> I also need to account for degeneracy in the support: a single facet gives rank 1 and predicts null dimension 6, which over-predicts. With n facets, the null dimension is capped below by 7 minus n. A plane requires at least 3 independent facet rows to correctly read dimension 4, and since all facets of a plane share the same normal but differ in position, the rows do span a rank-3 subspace.
> 
> For a plane specifically, there's a subtlety in how the rows behave: the row space forms a 2-dimensional affine subspace within the 7-dimensional space as the facet centroids vary across the plane.
> 
> Now I'm realizing the rank-nullity picture: a cylinder needs rank 5 (null dimension 2), which means you'd need around 5 facets to distinguish it from a plane. With only 2 facets, the rank is at most 2, giving null dimension ≥ 5—which reads as "more degenerate than a plane." This is actually the honest behavior: at 2 facets you genuinely cannot distinguish the surface type, so the algorithm defaults to the simplest fit. That's the intended design, but it means measuring a cylinder, cone, or torus properly requires roughly 5-6 facets, which is a real cost. That's exactly what the probe is meant to test. The key insight is that with fewer rows, the null space gets larger, so a smaller support actually gives a *simpler* reading than the true answer — which is exactly the fallback behavior the user wanted. The real question for probe 1 is whether the method locks onto the correct answer as support increases, and whether it passes through wrong intermediate readings like misidentifying cylinder data as cone at 5 facets. - Taubin, G. / Pratt, V. — direct algebraic surface fitting, the source of the
