## Nested fitting: a rank ladder instead of a type ladder (surveyed 2026-08-06)

The question this section answers: **could one fitter, run on a growing patch,
return "the simplest surface that explains what I have so far" — plane, then
cylinder, then cone, then sphere, then torus — with the type an *output* rather
than a precondition?** Asked because that is the missing piece of Phase 1.2. The
earn loop died on a chicken-and-egg — "a cone cannot be fitted from the two
facets a validated loop has when it must first decide, so it never grows" — and
a fitter that never has to decide first does not have that problem.

This is a survey with the option set, the costs, and the two families that are
*not* the answer. The rank-ladder claim below is a hypothesis with a probe
attached (`--invariant-report` family K, "The rank ladder measured" below), not
a settled result.

### The nesting must come from tangency, not from algebra

The obvious nested family is the **general quadric**, and it is the wrong one for
this pipeline, for two reasons that are already measured here rather than
speculative.

It **omits the torus**, the one surface every fillet needs, while including
ellipsoids, paraboloids and hyperboloids that CAD essentially never emits. On
support that barely pins a circular cylinder, the best algebraic quadric is
generically some elliptic cylinder or hyperboloid. Escalating to a quartic to
capture the torus is strictly worse: 34 coefficients, ≥34 points, degree-8
moments, and an even larger zoo of irrelevant surfaces. No reverse-engineering
work in the literature fits tori that way.

And it is **points-only, which is exactly the channel that cannot discriminate**.
The Invariants section measured that a wrong type explains a true support at the
same residual as the right one — a sphere fits 393 of 397 true cylinders *better*
than the cylinder does, at 0.2–0.5 float32 quanta. A points-only algebraic fitter
inherits that blindness whole. Type-projecting the result does not repair it:
a right circular cone requires a repeated eigenvalue in `M`, a condition Andrews
& Séquin note is neither linear nor quadratic in the quadric coefficients, so it
cannot be imposed on the linear fit (this is Option B under "Cone
initialization", with its ≥9 points and its algebraic-distance bias).

What nests correctly is the **space of tangent velocity fields**. Fit the
instantaneous similarity field

```text
v(p) = γ p + c × p + c̄        (7 unknowns: dilation γ, rotation c, translation c̄)
```

and require it tangent to the surface, `v(p)·n(p) = 0`. Each oriented facet
contributes **one linear row**, homogeneous:

```text
γ (p·n) + c·(p × n) + c̄·n = 0
```

This is Pottmann's kinematic surface fitting as generalized by Andrews & Séquin,
both already cited above. What makes it the answer to the question is not the
solve but the **dimension of its null space**, which is a property of the surface
and nothing else:

| surface | tangent similarity fields | null dim | rank | facets to reach it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| plane | 2 in-plane translations, rotation about the normal, one dilation | **4** | 3 | 3 |
| sphere | 3 rotations about the centre | **3** | 4 | 4 |
| cylinder | rotation about + translation along the axis | **2** | 5 | 5 |
| cone | rotation about the axis + **dilation from the apex** | **2** | 5 | 5 |
| torus / any surface of revolution | rotation about the axis | **1** | 6 | 6 |
| extrusion | translation along the generator | **1** | 6 | 6 |
| freeform | none | **0** | 7 | 7 |

**The dimension order is the simplicity order**, and it puts the sphere between
plane and cylinder rather than in a separate branch. Under-determination behaves
correctly for free: with `k` rows the rank is at most `k`, so the null space is at
*least* `7 − k`-dimensional, and a patch that has not yet earned its type reads as
a simpler surface than it is. That is precisely "when there's insufficient
support to pin down the result, default to the simplest surface that fits" — as a
**rank statement rather than a residual statement**, which matters because the
residual demonstrably cannot carry it.

Dimension alone does not separate cylinder from cone; the null space's *content*
does, and reading it costs nothing:

| reading | type |
|---|---|
| dim 4 | plane |
| dim 3 | sphere |
| dim 2, every null vector has `γ = 0` | cylinder |
| dim 2, some null vector has `γ ≠ 0` | **cone** — the dilation from the apex |
| dim 1, `c ≠ 0` | torus / surface of revolution |
| dim 1, `c = 0` | extrusion |
| dim 0 | freeform |

Parameters come out of the surviving generators: the rotation generator's Plücker
line **is** the axis, and `A = −c̄/γ` on the cone branch is the apex.

### Why this is cheap here specifically

**Three of the four Phase 1.1 initializers are already sub-blocks of this one
system**, which is the strongest argument that the unification is real rather
than decorative:

- The torus's meridian solve `qᵢ·d + nᵢ·m = 0` (`axis_line_from_normals`) is the
  **rigid sub-block**, `γ = 0`, with `(d, m) = (c, c̄)` read as a Plücker line.
- Cone Option A's `γ(p·n) + a·n = 0` is the **scaling sub-block**, `c = 0`.
- `cylinder()`'s `n₁ × n₂` and `sphere()`'s concurrent circumcenter normal lines
  are 2-row closed forms of the same structure.

The 7×7 Gram matrix *reads which structure is present* instead of assuming one
per solve. It is also **incremental** — one rank-1 update per admitted face, one
7×7 symmetric eigensolve per promotion check — which the per-type LM fitters can
never be, so the check can afford to run at every face admission.

Every measured lesson above transfers unchanged: normalize rows to the patch's own
centroid and extent (the scale-mismatch note under the torus construction — and
the normalization is provably rank-preserving, since translating and scaling the
coordinates maps the 7-parameter space to itself invertibly, so it is conditioning
and never an answer); select the facets to *initialize* from by ruling alignment
and angular spread while *collection* stays validation-gated (the
similarity-vs-spread separation Phase 1.2 paid for); and treat the result as a
**seed for the vertex-based least squares**, never as the answer, because every
row is built from a facet normal and inherits the `O(Δθ²/8)` chord-plane lean.

**Why the facet centroid is the right evaluation point**, and it is not obvious:
the row wants a point on the surface paired with the true normal, and the facet
gives neither exactly. For a ruling-aligned quad on a cylinder the two errors
cancel — the chord of a circular arc is parallel to the tangent at the arc's
*midpoint*, and the centroid sits at that midpoint angle, so the rotation
generator annihilates the row exactly. Evaluating at a vertex instead is `O(Δθ)`
wrong, an order worse. On a cone the facet plane contains the apex exactly, so the
`p·n` entry is exactly zero however the facet is shaped. On a **sphere** the exact
point is the facet's **circumcenter** rather than its centroid, since that is
where the normal line passes through the centre — the same distinction Phase 1.1
recorded for `sphere()`. Both are measured under family K below.

### What it does not fix

Stated plainly, because the failure modes are known and the rank ladder does not
touch them:

- **Genuine ambiguity stays ambiguous.** A single strip band — vertices on two
  circles, every facet centroid at one axial position — is sphere-compatible in
  the normal channel as well as the vertex channel (`simple_cylinder`'s 32-quad
  band, residual 2.07e-7 mm). The rank signal is degenerate there for the same
  reason the residual is. The load stays on the structural gates
  (`MIN_AXIAL_SWEEP_DEG`, solid-angle coverage) and on branch-don't-choose.
- **Rank is not to be read with a threshold.** The eigenvalue spectrum orders
  candidate interpretations; **validation decides**. This is the same rule
  `MIN_NULLSPACE_SEPARATION` is documented under, arrived at from the other end —
  the measured sphere-vs-torus conditioning gap is 2.06e-6 against 9.2e-4, a
  factor of 450 with pure data-dependence in between, so no cut exists.
- **Support cost is real and is the headline risk.** A cylinder needs 5
  independent rows against the 2 facets `initialize::cylinder` needs, a torus 6
  against a vertex star's 4–12. The rank ladder is a *classifier* that needs more
  support than the closed forms need, and whether the extra support is available
  before a patch crosses a junction is the whole question family K asks.
- **Plane starvation of the freeform rung.** An earned plane patch is by
  construction "the faces that validate a plane", so a single-facet plane always
  succeeds — measured to collapse the NURBS models to 94/51/83 per-facet planes.
  Whatever consumes the ladder must keep the leftover-coherence policy.

### The option set, ranked

- **A — the kinematic rank ladder above.** Recommended backbone. Adds the normal
  channel the points-only methods lack, subsumes three existing initializers,
  incremental, and its degeneracies are the ones already understood.
- **B — general quadric with eigen-projection.** Demoted to triage
  (analytic-vs-freeform) and initializer of last resort, for the reasons above.
- **C — per-type fitters with degeneracy-safe parameterizations.** The
  conservative option, and *complementary rather than alternative*: adopt
  Lukács–Martin–Marshall's parameterization discipline, whose stated property is
  that "as the principal curvatures decrease, the results naturally become closer
  to the surfaces of simpler type, unlike other methods which may diverge". That
  is the same move as this document's cone `taper = tan α` and the bent tube's
  `k = 1/R`. Keep it in the refiners whatever backbone wins; it leaves the four
  initializers un-unified and promotion hand-enumerated if used alone.
- **D — Dupin cyclides.** The one classical family containing *exactly*
  {plane, sphere, cylinder, cone, torus} and closed under the relevant
  degenerations, with a genuine unified theory in the space of oriented spheres.
  Declined: the literature is construction- and blend-oriented, mesh fitting is
  thin, the Minkowski–Lorentz machinery is heavy, and the extra coverage is a
  liability in a pipeline whose whole discipline is "simplest surface that
  validates". Worth remembering only if variable-radius blends become a target.

### The rank ladder measured (family K)

`src/stage2/kinematic.rs` builds the system; `--invariant-report` family K scores
it against `--compare` ground truth. Per reference face, over a BFS patch grown
from several starts and sampled at a ladder of support sizes: the 7 relative
eigenvalues, the null dimension read from the widest ratio gap, the type that
dimension plus its null-space content names, whether that matches the truth, the
straddling fraction of the support, and — the analogue of family F — the residual
of the **reference's own** tangent generators against the measured rows, at both
the centroid and the circumcenter, which asks whether the signal is there to be
read before asking whether the read succeeds.

//! The kinematic rank ladder: one linear system whose **null-space dimension**
//! names the surface type, so that "the simplest surface that fits the support
//! so far" is a rank statement rather than a residual statement.
//!
//! See SURFACE_FITTING.md, "Nested fitting: a rank ladder instead of a type
//! ladder", for the survey this implements and for why the alternatives (general
//! quadric, quartic, Dupin cyclide) were declined.
//!
//! # The system
//!
//! Fit the instantaneous similarity velocity field
//!
//! ```text
//! v(p) = γ p + c × p + c̄
//! ```
//!
//! and require it tangent to the surface, `v(p)·n(p) = 0`. Each oriented facet
//! gives one homogeneous linear row in the seven unknowns `(γ, c, c̄)`:
//!
//! ```text
//! γ (p·n) + c·(p × n) + c̄·n = 0
//! ```
//!
//! The set of similarity fields tangent to a surface is a property of the
//! surface, and its dimension is the ladder:
//!
//! | surface | null dim | rank | rows needed |
//! |---|---|---|---|
//! | plane | 4 | 3 | 3 |
//! | sphere | 3 | 4 | 4 |
//! | cylinder | 2 | 5 | 5 |
//! | cone | 2 | 5 | 5 |
//! | torus / surface of revolution | 1 | 6 | 6 |
//! | freeform | 0 | 7 | 7 |
//!
//! Under-determination therefore behaves correctly with no special case: `k`
//! rows can only pin rank `k`, so a patch that has not yet earned its type reads
//! as something simpler. Cylinder and cone share a dimension and are separated by
//! the null space's *content* — a cone admits a dilation (`γ ≠ 0`) from its apex
//! and a cylinder does not.
//!
//! # What this module is and is not
//!
//! It is a **classifier and an initializer**, never a validator. Every row is
//! built from a facet normal, which carries the `O(Δθ²/8)` chord-plane lean, so a
//! parameter read out of here is a seed for the vertex-based least squares and
//! not an answer — the lesson Phase 1.2 paid for. Nothing here decides
//! membership.
//!
//! There is deliberately **no threshold that converts a spectrum into a type**.
//! [`Spectrum::null_dim_by_widest_gap`] reads a dimension for reporting and for
//! *ordering* candidate interpretations; validation decides which one lives. See
//! `initialize::MIN_NULLSPACE_SEPARATION` for the measurement that says no such
//! threshold exists.

// Phase K is instrumented before it is wired in: today the only callers are the
// unit tests and the family-K probe in `invariants.rs`.
#![allow(dead_code)]

use nalgebra::{SMatrix, SVector};

use super::{cross3, dot3, face_centroid, normalize3};
use crate::stage1::ConnectedMesh;

type Mat7 = SMatrix<f64, 7, 7>;
type Vec7 = SVector<f64, 7>;

/// Where on a facet the tangency constraint is evaluated.
///
/// The row wants a point *on the surface* paired with the *true* normal, and a
/// facet supplies neither exactly. Which point makes the two errors cancel
/// depends on the surface, which is why this is a parameter rather than a
/// constant:
///
/// - [`Eval::Centroid`] is exact for a ruling-aligned quad on a cylinder or
///   cone. The chord of a circular arc is parallel to the tangent at the arc's
///   *midpoint*, and the centroid sits at that midpoint angle, so the rotation
///   generator annihilates the row exactly. A vertex would be `O(Δθ)` wrong.
/// - [`Eval::Circumcenter`] is exact for a facet inscribed in a sphere, where
///   the normal line through the circumcenter passes through the centre — the
///   construction `initialize::sphere` already relies on.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub(crate) enum Eval {
    Centroid,
    Circumcenter,
}

/// The frame a system was normalized in, needed to map a recovered field back.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy)]
pub(crate) struct Frame {
    pub origin: [f64; 3],
    pub extent: f64,
}

/// The accumulated `7×7` Gram matrix `Σ rowᵢ rowᵢᵀ` and its frame.
///
/// Built incrementally — one rank-1 update per facet — which is the property
/// that lets a promotion check run at every face admission.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub(crate) struct System {
    m: Mat7,
    frame: Frame,
    rows: usize,
}

/// The circumcenter of a facet's vertices, in the facet's own plane.
///
/// Least squares over the facet's vertices, so it is the exact circumcenter for
/// a triangle and the best-fit circle centre for a fused quad (whose vertices
/// are concyclic when they lie on a sphere, since a plane meets a sphere in a
/// circle).
fn facet_circumcenter(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, fi: usize) -> Option<[f64; 3]> {
    let face = &mesh.faces[fi];
    let n = face.vertex_count as usize;
    if n < 3 {
        return None;
    }
    let pts: Vec<[f64; 3]> = (0..n)
        .map(|k| {
            let v = &mesh.vertices[face.vertex_indices[k]];
            [v.x, v.y, v.z]
        })
        .collect();
    let mut centre = [0.0_f64; 3];
    for p in &pts {
        for k in 0..3 {
            centre[k] += p[k] / n as f64;
        }
    }
    let mut normal = face.normal?;
    if normalize3(&mut normal) == 0.0 {
        return None;
    }
    // An orthonormal basis of the facet plane, from its longest edge so the
    // frame is well conditioned on a sliver.
    let mut e1 = [
        pts[1][0] - pts[0][0],
        pts[1][1] - pts[0][1],
        pts[1][2] - pts[0][2],
    ];
    if normalize3(&mut e1) == 0.0 {
        return None;
    }
    let e2 = cross3(&normal, &e1);

    // |p - c|² equal for all p: subtract pairwise to linearize in c.
    // 2(pᵢ - p₀)·c = |pᵢ|² - |p₀|², in plane coordinates about the mean.
    let local: Vec<(f64, f64)> = pts
        .iter()
        .map(|p| {
            let w = [p[0] - centre[0], p[1] - centre[1], p[2] - centre[2]];
            (dot3(&w, &e1), dot3(&w, &e2))
        })
        .collect();
    let mut ata = nalgebra::Matrix2::<f64>::zeros();
    let mut atb = nalgebra::Vector2::<f64>::zeros();
    let (x0, y0) = local[0];
    for &(x, y) in &local[1..] {
        let row = nalgebra::Vector2::new(2.0 * (x - x0), 2.0 * (y - y0));
        let rhs = (x * x + y * y) - (x0 * x0 + y0 * y0);
        ata += row * row.transpose();
        atb += row * rhs;
    }
    let sol = ata.lu().solve(&atb)?;
    if !sol.iter().all(|v| v.is_finite()) {
        return None;
    }
    Some([
        centre[0] + e1[0] * sol[0] + e2[0] * sol[1],
        centre[1] + e1[1] * sol[0] + e2[1] * sol[1],
        centre[2] + e1[2] * sol[0] + e2[2] * sol[1],
    ])
}

/// The evaluation point and unit normal a facet contributes.
fn facet_sample(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, fi: usize, eval: Eval) -> Option<([f64; 3], [f64; 3])> {
    let mut n = mesh.faces[fi].normal?;
    if normalize3(&mut n) == 0.0 {
        return None;
    }
    let p = match eval {
        Eval::Centroid => face_centroid(&mesh.faces[fi], &mesh.vertices),
        Eval::Circumcenter => facet_circumcenter(mesh, fi)?,
    };
    Some((p, n))
}

impl System {
    /// Build the system for `faces`, normalized to their own centroid and
    /// extent.
    ///
    /// **Normalization is provably rank-preserving.** Under `p = o + L p'` the
    /// field `γp + c×p + c̄` becomes `L(γp' + c×p') + (γo + c×o + c̄)`, so in the
    /// primed coordinates the parameters are `(γ, c, (γo + c×o + c̄)/L)` — an
    /// invertible linear map of the 7-parameter space onto itself, leaving unit
    /// normals untouched. So this changes conditioning and never an answer,
    /// which matters because the `γ` and `c` blocks carry length units and the
    /// `c̄` block does not: on a patch far from the origin the null space would
    /// otherwise drown in the scale mismatch rather than in the data. This is
    /// the same defect, and the same fix, as `initialize::axis_line_from_normals`.
    pub(crate) fn build(faces: &[usize], mesh: &ConnectedMesh, eval: Eval) -> Option<System> {
        let samples: Vec<([f64; 3], [f64; 3])> = faces
            .iter()
            .filter_map(|&fi| facet_sample(mesh, fi, eval))
            .collect();
        if samples.is_empty() {
            return None;
        }
        let inv = 1.0 / samples.len() as f64;
        let mut origin = [0.0_f64; 3];
        for (p, _) in &samples {
            for k in 0..3 {
                origin[k] += p[k] * inv;
            }
        }
        let mut var = 0.0_f64;
        for (p, _) in &samples {
            let w = [p[0] - origin[0], p[1] - origin[1], p[2] - origin[2]];
            var += dot3(&w, &w) * inv;
        }
        // A single facet, or a patch whose points coincide, has no extent of its
        // own to normalize by. Fall back to unit scale: the system is still
        // correct, just conditioned by the caller's units.
        let extent = if var > 0.0 { var.sqrt() } else { 1.0 };
        if !extent.is_finite() {
            return None;
        }
        let frame = Frame { origin, extent };

        let mut m = Mat7::zeros();
        let mut rows = 0usize;
        for (p, n) in &samples {
            let row = Self::row(*p, *n, frame);
            m += row * row.transpose();
            rows += 1;
        }
        Some(System { m, frame, rows })
    }

    /// One facet's row `[p·n, (p × n), n]` in the normalized frame.
    fn row(p: [f64; 3], n: [f64; 3], frame: Frame) -> Vec7 {
        let local = [
            (p[0] - frame.origin[0]) / frame.extent,
            (p[1] - frame.origin[1]) / frame.extent,
            (p[2] - frame.origin[2]) / frame.extent,
        ];
        let q = cross3(&local, &n);
        Vec7::from_row_slice(&[dot3(&local, &n), q[0], q[1], q[2], n[0], n[1], n[2]])
    }

    pub(crate) fn rows(&self) -> usize {
        self.rows
    }

    pub(crate) fn frame(&self) -> Frame {
        self.frame
    }

    /// Add one more facet — the incremental path, for a patch that grows.
    pub(crate) fn push(&mut self, fi: usize, mesh: &ConnectedMesh, eval: Eval) {
        if let Some((p, n)) = facet_sample(mesh, fi, eval) {
            let row = Self::row(p, n, self.frame);
            self.m += row * row.transpose();
            self.rows += 1;
        }
    }

    pub(crate) fn spectrum(&self) -> Spectrum {
        let eigen = nalgebra::SymmetricEigen::new(self.m);
        let mut order: Vec<usize> = (0..7).collect();
        order.sort_by(|&a, &b| eigen.eigenvalues[a].total_cmp(&eigen.eigenvalues[b]));
        let mut values = [0.0_f64; 7];
        let mut vectors = Mat7::zeros();
        for (slot, &src) in order.iter().enumerate() {
            values[slot] = eigen.eigenvalues[src].max(0.0);
            vectors.set_column(slot, &eigen.eigenvectors.column(src));
        }
        Spectrum {
            values,
            vectors,
            rows: self.rows,
            frame: self.frame,
        }
    }

    /// `‖M v‖ / λ_max` for a unit `v` — how well a *claimed* tangent field is
    /// annihilated by the measured rows, relative to the system's own scale.
    ///
    /// This is the family-K analogue of family F: fed the reference surface's own
    /// generators it asks whether the signal is there to be read, before asking
    /// whether the read succeeds.
    pub(crate) fn generator_residual(&self, v: Vec7) -> f64 {
        let scale = self.m.trace();
        if !(scale > 0.0) {
            return f64::NAN;
        }
        let mut u = v;
        let norm = u.norm();
        if !(norm > 0.0) {
            return f64::NAN;
        }
        u /= norm;
        (self.m * u).norm() / scale
    }
}

/// The eigen-decomposition of a [`System`], ascending.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub(crate) struct Spectrum {
    /// Eigenvalues, ascending. The null space is at the low end.
    pub values: [f64; 7],
    /// Matching eigenvectors, column `i` for `values[i]`.
    pub vectors: Mat7,
    pub rows: usize,
    pub frame: Frame,
}

/// What a null space of a given dimension and content names.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub(crate) enum KinematicType {
    /// Fewer independent rows than the ladder needs — nothing is pinned yet.
    Underdetermined,
    Plane,
    Sphere,
    Cylinder,
    Cone,
    /// A rotation survives: torus, or any other surface of revolution.
    Revolution,
    /// A translation survives and no rotation does.
    Extrusion,
    /// One field survives carrying a dilation — a spiral or conical helix.
    Spiral,
    Freeform,
}

impl KinematicType {
    pub(crate) fn name(self) -> &'static str {
        match self {
            KinematicType::Underdetermined => "under",
            KinematicType::Plane => "plane",
            KinematicType::Sphere => "sphere",
            KinematicType::Cylinder => "cylinder",
            KinematicType::Cone => "cone",
            KinematicType::Revolution => "revolution",
            KinematicType::Extrusion => "extrusion",
            KinematicType::Spiral => "spiral",
            KinematicType::Freeform => "freeform",
        }
    }
}

/// Below this share of a null vector's norm, a block is numerical drift rather
/// than structure.
///
/// A validity floor on a *unit* vector's own components, not a fit quality bar:
/// it decides whether a generator has a dilation or a rotation part at all. The
/// two cases it separates are structural — a cylinder's null space has `γ`
/// identically zero by construction, a cone's has `γ` of order one — so any cut
/// well below 1 gives the same answer.
const BLOCK_PRESENT: f64 = 1.0e-6;

impl Spectrum {
    /// The null dimension implied by the widest *ratio* gap in the spectrum.
    ///
    /// Reported, and used to order candidate interpretations. **Not a decision.**
    /// The spectrum is scale-free after normalization, but the gap it shows is
    /// data-dependent — see `initialize::MIN_NULLSPACE_SEPARATION` for the
    /// measurement that killed the idea of thresholding one.
    ///
    /// Returns the dimension and the ratio that produced it, so a caller can see
    /// how believable the reading is.
    pub(crate) fn null_dim_by_widest_gap(&self) -> (usize, f64) {
        let top = self.values[6];
        if !(top > 0.0) {
            return (7, f64::INFINITY);
        }
        // Guard against exact zeros: work in a floor-clamped log scale so a
        // 0-vs-tiny step is a real gap rather than an infinity.
        let floor = top * 1.0e-18;
        let mut best = (0usize, 0.0_f64);
        for k in 1..7 {
            let lo = self.values[k - 1].max(floor);
            let hi = self.values[k].max(floor);
            let ratio = hi / lo;
            if ratio > best.1 {
                best = (k, ratio);
            }
        }
        // A gap below index k means dimensions 0..k are null.
        if self.values[0].max(floor) / top > 1.0e-12 {
            // No eigenvalue is small enough to call null.
            return (0, best.1);
        }
        best
    }

    /// The type named by a null space of dimension `dim`.
    ///
    /// Dimension alone stops at cylinder-or-cone; the content separates them.
    /// `rows` bounds what can be claimed: `k` rows can pin at most rank `k`, so a
    /// dimension larger than `7 - rows` is under-determination rather than
    /// simplicity.
    pub(crate) fn classify(&self, dim: usize) -> KinematicType {
        if dim + self.rows > 7 && dim > 4 {
            return KinematicType::Underdetermined;
        }
        match dim {
            0 => KinematicType::Freeform,
            1 => {
                let v = self.vectors.column(0);
                let gamma = v[0].abs();
                let rot = (v[1] * v[1] + v[2] * v[2] + v[3] * v[3]).sqrt();
                if gamma > BLOCK_PRESENT {
                    KinematicType::Spiral
                } else if rot > BLOCK_PRESENT {
                    KinematicType::Revolution
                } else {
                    KinematicType::Extrusion
                }
            }
            2 => {
                // A cone's 2-space contains the dilation from its apex; a
                // cylinder's is rotation + translation along the axis, with γ
                // identically zero. Test the whole subspace, not one basis
                // vector: the eigenvectors are an arbitrary basis of it.
                let gamma = (0..2)
                    .map(|k| self.vectors[(0, k)].abs())
                    .fold(0.0_f64, f64::max);
                if gamma > BLOCK_PRESENT {
                    KinematicType::Cone
                } else {
                    KinematicType::Cylinder
                }
            }
            3 => KinematicType::Sphere,
            4 => KinematicType::Plane,
            _ => KinematicType::Underdetermined,
        }
    }

    /// The type the spectrum reads on its own.
    pub(crate) fn read(&self) -> (KinematicType, usize, f64) {
        let (dim, ratio) = self.null_dim_by_widest_gap();
        (self.classify(dim), dim, ratio)
    }
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// The reference's own generators, for family K's satisfiability question
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// A tangent similarity field in the *world* frame, mapped into a system's
/// normalized frame so it can be tested against the measured rows.
///
/// The map is the one derived in [`System::build`]: `(γ, c, c̄)` in world
/// coordinates becomes `(γ, c, (γ o + c × o + c̄) / L)` in the frame.
pub(crate) fn generator_in_frame(gamma: f64, c: [f64; 3], cbar: [f64; 3], frame: Frame) -> Vec7 {
    let co = cross3(&c, &frame.origin);
    let t = [
        (gamma * frame.origin[0] + co[0] + cbar[0]) / frame.extent,
        (gamma * frame.origin[1] + co[1] + cbar[1]) / frame.extent,
        (gamma * frame.origin[2] + co[2] + cbar[2]) / frame.extent,
    ];
    Vec7::from_row_slice(&[gamma, c[0], c[1], c[2], t[0], t[1], t[2]])
}

/// Rotation about the line through `pos` with direction `dir`: `v(p) = d × (p − a)`.
pub(crate) fn rotation_about(pos: [f64; 3], dir: [f64; 3], frame: Frame) -> Vec7 {
    let mut d = dir;
    normalize3(&mut d);
    // d × (p − a) = d × p + (a × d), so c = d and c̄ = a × d.
    let cbar = cross3(&pos, &d);
    generator_in_frame(0.0, d, cbar, frame)
}

/// Translation along `dir`.
pub(crate) fn translation_along(dir: [f64; 3], frame: Frame) -> Vec7 {
    let mut d = dir;
    normalize3(&mut d);
    generator_in_frame(0.0, [0.0; 3], d, frame)
}

/// Dilation about `centre`: `v(p) = p − a`.
pub(crate) fn dilation_about(centre: [f64; 3], frame: Frame) -> Vec7 {
    generator_in_frame(1.0, [0.0; 3], [-centre[0], -centre[1], -centre[2]], frame)
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Tests
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;
    use crate::stage1::{MeshFace, MeshVertex};

    /// An oblique unit axis: an axis-aligned one would let a sign or an index
    /// slip through every assertion here.
    fn oblique_axis() -> [f64; 3] {
        let mut d = [0.3, -0.5, 0.81];
        normalize3(&mut d);
        d
    }

    /// An origin well away from the coordinate origin, so the normalization is
    /// actually exercised — an untranslated patch would pass even with the
    /// scaling bug the frame exists to prevent.
    fn far_origin() -> [f64; 3] {
        [120.0, -45.0, 260.0]
    }

    fn mesh_from(points: Vec<[f64; 3]>, faces: Vec<Vec<usize>>) -> ConnectedMesh {
        let vertices: Vec<MeshVertex> = points
            .iter()
            .map(|p| MeshVertex::from_xyz(p[0], p[1], p[2]))
            .collect();
        let faces = faces
            .into_iter()
            .map(|idx| {
                // The Newell normal, exactly as stage 1 computes it.
                let mut n = [0.0_f64; 3];
                for i in 0..idx.len() {
                    let c = points[idx[i]];
                    let x = points[idx[(i + 1) % idx.len()]];
                    n[0] += (c[1] - x[1]) * (c[2] + x[2]);
                    n[1] += (c[2] - x[2]) * (c[0] + x[0]);
                    n[2] += (c[0] - x[0]) * (c[1] + x[1]);
                }
                normalize3(&mut n);
                let mut vertex_indices = [0usize; 4];
                vertex_indices[..idx.len()].copy_from_slice(&idx);
                MeshFace {
                    vertex_count: idx.len() as u8,
                    vertex_indices,
                    neighbors: [-1; 4],
                    normal: Some(n),
                }
            })
            .collect();
        ConnectedMesh {
            vertices,
            faces,
            stats: Default::default(),
        }
    }

    fn perp_basis(d: [f64; 3]) -> ([f64; 3], [f64; 3]) {
        super::super::perpendicular_basis(&d)
    }

    /// A ruling-aligned quad strip: `rings` circles of `divs` points about the
    /// axis, at radius `radius(z)`.
    fn revolution_strip(
        origin: [f64; 3],
        axis: [f64; 3],
        zs: &[f64],
        divs: usize,
        radius: impl Fn(f64) -> f64,
    ) -> ConnectedMesh {
        let (u, v) = perp_basis(axis);
        let mut points = Vec::new();
        for &z in zs {
            let r = radius(z);
            for k in 0..divs {
                let t = std::f64::consts::TAU * k as f64 / divs as f64;
                let (c, s) = (t.cos(), t.sin());
                points.push([
                    origin[0] + axis[0] * z + r * (c * u[0] + s * v[0]),
                    origin[1] + axis[1] * z + r * (c * u[1] + s * v[1]),
                    origin[2] + axis[2] * z + r * (c * u[2] + s * v[2]),
                ]);
            }
        }
        let mut faces = Vec::new();
        for ring in 0..zs.len() - 1 {
            for k in 0..divs - 1 {
                let a = ring * divs + k;
                let b = ring * divs + k + 1;
                faces.push(vec![a, b, b + divs, a + divs]);
            }
        }
        mesh_from(points, faces)
    }

    fn all(mesh: &ConnectedMesh) -> Vec<usize> {
        (0..mesh.faces.len()).collect()
    }

    fn read(mesh: &ConnectedMesh) -> (KinematicType, usize, f64) {
        System::build(&all(mesh), mesh, Eval::Centroid)
            .expect("system")
            .spectrum()
            .read()
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_plane_reads_null_dimension_four() {
        // Six triangles of a planar patch, deliberately irregular so no
        // symmetry can hide an error.
        let d = oblique_axis();
        let (u, v) = perp_basis(d);
        let o = far_origin();
        let pt = |s: f64, t: f64| {
            [
                o[0] + s * u[0] + t * v[0],
                o[1] + s * u[1] + t * v[1],
                o[2] + s * u[2] + t * v[2],
            ]
        };
        let points = vec![
            pt(0.0, 0.0),
            pt(3.0, 0.4),
            pt(1.1, 2.7),
            pt(-2.2, 1.9),
            pt(-1.7, -2.4),
            pt(2.3, -3.1),
        ];
        let mesh = mesh_from(
            points,
            vec![
                vec![0, 1, 2],
                vec![0, 2, 3],
                vec![0, 3, 4],
                vec![0, 4, 5],
                vec![0, 5, 1],
            ],
        );
        let (kind, dim, _) = read(&mesh);
        assert_eq!(dim, 4, "a plane's tangent similarity fields are 4-dimensional");
        assert_eq!(kind, KinematicType::Plane);
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_sphere_reads_null_dimension_three_at_the_circumcenter() {
        // A sphere patch as a quad grid in spherical coordinates. Its facets are
        // NOT equilateral, so centroid and circumcenter genuinely differ — which
        // is the property under test.
        let centre = far_origin();
        let radius = 7.0;
        let mut points = Vec::new();
        let (nu, nv) = (5usize, 6usize);
        for i in 0..nu {
            let phi = 0.6 + 0.9 * i as f64 / (nu - 1) as f64;
            for j in 0..nv {
                let th = 0.3 + 1.4 * j as f64 / (nv - 1) as f64;
                points.push([
                    centre[0] + radius * phi.sin() * th.cos(),
                    centre[1] + radius * phi.sin() * th.sin(),
                    centre[2] + radius * phi.cos(),
                ]);
            }
        }
        let mut faces = Vec::new();
        for i in 0..nu - 1 {
            for j in 0..nv - 1 {
                let a = i * nv + j;
                // Triangles: a quad on a sphere is not planar, so a quad facet
                // here would have a meaningless normal.
                faces.push(vec![a, a + 1, a + nv]);
                faces.push(vec![a + 1, a + nv + 1, a + nv]);
            }
        }
        let mesh = mesh_from(points, faces);

        let sys = System::build(&all(&mesh), &mesh, Eval::Circumcenter).expect("system");
        let (kind, dim, _) = sys.spectrum().read();
        assert_eq!(dim, 3, "a sphere's tangent fields are its three rotations");
        assert_eq!(kind, KinematicType::Sphere);

        // And the circumcenter is exact where the centroid is not: the true
        // generators are annihilated to machine precision by one and not the
        // other. This is the module's evaluation-point claim, in a test.
        let g = rotation_about(centre, oblique_axis(), sys.frame());
        let exact = sys.generator_residual(g);
        let centroid_sys = System::build(&all(&mesh), &mesh, Eval::Centroid).expect("system");
        let sloppy =
            centroid_sys.generator_residual(rotation_about(centre, oblique_axis(), centroid_sys.frame()));
        assert!(
            exact < 1.0e-14,
            "circumcenter rows annihilate a true rotation exactly, got {exact:e}"
        );
        assert!(
            sloppy > exact * 100.0,
            "the centroid should be measurably worse on a sphere: {sloppy:e} vs {exact:e}"
        );
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_cylinder_reads_null_dimension_two_with_no_dilation() {
        let axis = oblique_axis();
        let mesh = revolution_strip(far_origin(), axis, &[-3.0, 0.0, 2.5, 6.0], 16, |_| 4.0);
        let (kind, dim, _) = read(&mesh);
        assert_eq!(dim, 2, "rotation about the axis plus translation along it");
        assert_eq!(
            kind,
            KinematicType::Cylinder,
            "a cylinder's null space has no dilation in it"
        );
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_cone_reads_null_dimension_two_with_a_dilation() {
        let axis = oblique_axis();
        let apex = far_origin();
        // r = z tan α, so the apex is at z = 0 and the patch is a real cone.
        let tan_a = (18.0_f64).to_radians().tan();
        let mesh = revolution_strip(apex, axis, &[4.0, 7.0, 11.0, 16.0], 16, |z| z * tan_a);
        let (kind, dim, _) = read(&mesh);
        assert_eq!(dim, 2, "rotation about the axis plus dilation from the apex");
        assert_eq!(
            kind,
            KinematicType::Cone,
            "the dilation from the apex is what separates a cone from a cylinder"
        );
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_torus_reads_null_dimension_one() {
        let axis = oblique_axis();
        let centre = far_origin();
        let (u, v) = perp_basis(axis);
        let (major, minor) = (9.0, 2.0);
        let (nt, np) = (12usize, 9usize);
        let mut points = Vec::new();
        for i in 0..nt {
            let t = std::f64::consts::TAU * i as f64 / nt as f64;
            for j in 0..np {
                // A partial tube sweep, which is what a fillet actually is.
                let s = -0.9 + 1.8 * j as f64 / (np - 1) as f64;
                let rho = major + minor * s.cos();
                let z = minor * s.sin();
                points.push([
                    centre[0] + rho * (t.cos() * u[0] + t.sin() * v[0]) + z * axis[0],
                    centre[1] + rho * (t.cos() * u[1] + t.sin() * v[1]) + z * axis[1],
                    centre[2] + rho * (t.cos() * u[2] + t.sin() * v[2]) + z * axis[2],
                ]);
            }
        }
        let mut faces = Vec::new();
        for i in 0..nt - 1 {
            for j in 0..np - 1 {
                let a = i * np + j;
                faces.push(vec![a, a + np, a + np + 1, a + 1]);
            }
        }
        let mesh = mesh_from(points, faces);
        let (kind, dim, _) = read(&mesh);
        assert_eq!(dim, 1, "only rotation about the torus axis survives");
        assert_eq!(kind, KinematicType::Revolution);
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_reference_generators_are_annihilated_on_a_ruled_strip() {
        // The claim that makes the centroid the right evaluation point on a
        // developable: the chord of an arc is parallel to the tangent at the
        // arc's MIDPOINT, and a strip quad's centroid sits at that angle.
        let axis = oblique_axis();
        let pos = far_origin();
        let mesh = revolution_strip(pos, axis, &[-3.0, 0.0, 2.5, 6.0], 16, |_| 4.0);
        let sys = System::build(&all(&mesh), &mesh, Eval::Centroid).expect("system");
        let rot = sys.generator_residual(rotation_about(pos, axis, sys.frame()));
        let tra = sys.generator_residual(translation_along(axis, sys.frame()));
        assert!(rot < 1.0e-14, "rotation about the true axis, got {rot:e}");
        assert!(tra < 1.0e-14, "translation along the true axis, got {tra:e}");

        // And a cone's apex dilation, on the same construction.
        let apex = far_origin();
        let tan_a = (18.0_f64).to_radians().tan();
        let cone = revolution_strip(apex, axis, &[4.0, 7.0, 11.0, 16.0], 16, |z| z * tan_a);
        let csys = System::build(&all(&cone), &cone, Eval::Centroid).expect("system");
        let dil = csys.generator_residual(dilation_about(apex, csys.frame()));
        assert!(
            dil < 1.0e-14,
            "every facet plane of a cone contains the apex, got {dil:e}"
        );
    }

    #[test]
    fn support_below_the_rank_the_type_needs_reads_simpler() {
        // The property the whole ladder rests on: too few rows cannot pin a
        // type, and what comes back is a SIMPLER surface rather than a wrong
        // one. Two facets of a cylinder read as a plane's dimension or more.
        let axis = oblique_axis();
        let mesh = revolution_strip(far_origin(), axis, &[-3.0, 0.0, 2.5, 6.0], 16, |_| 4.0);
        let two = vec![0usize, 1usize];
        let sys = System::build(&two, &mesh, Eval::Centroid).expect("system");
        let (_, dim, _) = sys.spectrum().read();
        assert!(
            dim >= 5,
            "two rows can pin at most rank 2, so the null space is 5-D or more; got {dim}"
        );
        assert_eq!(
            sys.spectrum().classify(dim),
            KinematicType::Underdetermined,
            "and that must be reported as under-determination, not as a type"
        );

        // Five facets is the cylinder's rank, and it is reached from a strip
        // that spans two rings.
        let five: Vec<usize> = (0..5).collect();
        let sys5 = System::build(&five, &mesh, Eval::Centroid).expect("system");
        let (kind5, dim5, _) = sys5.spectrum().read();
        assert_eq!(dim5, 2, "five independent rows pin the cylinder");
        assert_eq!(kind5, KinematicType::Cylinder);
    }

    #[test]
    fn normalization_does_not_change_the_reading() {
        // The rank-preservation argument in `System::build`, as a test: the same
        // cylinder a metre from the origin reads identically.
        let axis = oblique_axis();
        let near = revolution_strip([0.0, 0.0, 0.0], axis, &[-3.0, 0.0, 2.5, 6.0], 16, |_| 4.0);
        let far = revolution_strip(
            [1000.0, -2000.0, 4000.0],
            axis,
            &[-3.0, 0.0, 2.5, 6.0],
            16,
            |_| 4.0,
        );
        let (kn, dn, _) = read(&near);
        let (kf, df, _) = read(&far);
        assert_eq!((kn, dn), (kf, df), "a translated patch must read the same");
    }
}

> Seven of the eight test cases pass, confirming the exact-data claims hold up. The one failure traces back to my test setup itself—it creates a degenerate case where faces 0 through 5 of the strip all occupy the same ring at a single axial position, which is exactly the "single strip band" degeneracy I already documented. These five quads sit at the same height, so I need to think through what the row span actually covers in this scenario.
> 
> The quads span from z=-3 to z=0, so they do have axial extent—each quad covers two rings. The faces are indexed as `ring * ... for ring in 0..15, for k in 0..5`, meaning faces 0 through 4 are all from ring 0 with k values 0 through 4, giving me five consecutive quads that cover only 5/16 of the circumference at one axial band. The dimension reading shows 4, which suggests rank 3, but five adjacent quads in one band around a cylinder should genuinely have rank 5. The real question is whether the widest-gap heuristic is picking the wrong location, so I need to check the actual spectrum to see what's happening. Rather than guess, I should measure this directly and examine what probe 1 reveals. This feels like an important finding beyond just a test artifact—I want to understand whether a single band of quads on a cylinder, where all quads span the same two rings, actually has rank 5.
> 
> Now I'm working through the geometry of a single band: the centroid lies at a fixed axial position z_c for all quads, with radial distance r_c from the axis, and the normal vector points radially outward. Computing the row entries—the dot product p·n gives a constant r_c, the cross product p×n produces a term proportional to z_c that varies with angle, and the normal itself varies with angle—these angular dependencies create a curve in a subspace where the θ-dependent parts are linked through the relationships between the cross product and normal components.
> 
> The row vector is actually affine in just two parameters: the x and y components of the unit normal (cosθ and sinθ), plus the constant r_c term. This means the row space is spanned by exactly three vectors—one for the constant part and two for the angular variations—giving a rank of 3, not 5. So a single axial band of a cylinder has the same kinematic rank as a plane, which is a significant constraint.
> 
> To achieve full rank 5, I need measurements from at least two distinct axial heights. With two bands at different z positions, the (q_x, q_y) and (n_x, n_y) blocks decouple properly, and the rank becomes 5. This confirms the "single strip band" degeneracy I noted earlier is even more fundamental than I initially described—the cylinder genuinely requires multiple axial facets to be fully constrained.
> 
> My test with 4 rings across 3 bands passed as expected, which validates this analysis. This is a key derived result that affects the minimum support requirements: a cylinder needs rank 5, which demands at least 2 axial bands and sufficient circumferential coverage. Let me recalculate the minimum support needed across different shapes.
> 
> For a cylinder to achieve rank 5, I need to verify empirically whether 2 bands with 2 circumferential positions gives rank 4 (reading as sphere-like) or if I need 5 facets spread across the bands. I'll modify my test to explicitly assert the single-band collapse as a pinned property, then use a cross-band selection to confirm the 5-facet case works correctly. The face indexing follows the pattern of ring position times divisions plus the circumferential offset.

    fn scratch_band_spectrum() {

        let mesh = revolution_strip(far_origin(), axis, &[-3.0, 0.0, 2.5, 6.0], 16, |_| 4.0);

        // The ladder's sharpest limitation, and it is structural rather than
        // numerical. In a band of ruling-aligned quads every centroid sits at
        // the same axial position `z_c` and the same radial distance `r_c`, so
        //
        //     p·n = r_c                      (the same constant for every facet)
        //     p × n = z_c (d × n)            (a fixed multiple of the normal)
        //
        // and every row is an affine function of `n` alone. The row space is
        // spanned by {constant, n_x, n_y} — **rank 3, whatever the arc** — so a
        // band reads as a plane no matter how far around the cylinder it goes.
        // The band carries no axial information at all: it is "a short arc
        // cannot determine a surface of revolution" applied to the axial
        // direction instead of the circumferential one.

        // the same conclusion the measured seed rules reached for the torus.
        let axis = oblique_axis();
        let mesh = revolution_strip(far_origin(), axis, &[-3.0, 0.0, 2.5, 6.0], 16, |_| 4.0);

        }

#!/bin/bash
# Invariant harness: run \`--invariant-report\` over every corpus model that has
# a reference STEP, and concatenate the per-reference-face TSV rows.
#
# Reuses tools/sweep.sh's model/reference pairing (\`--list\`) so the two
# instruments always measure the same corpus.
#
# Usage:
#   tools/invariants.sh [--out FILE] [--jobs N] [--filter REGEX] [--timeout SECS]
#                       [--seeds N] [--no-compete]
#
# Output TSV columns are documented in src/stage2/invariants.rs. Per-model
# stderr summaries go to <out>.log.

set -uo pipefail
cd "$(dirname "$0")/.."

BREPPER=${BREPPER:-./target/release/brepper}
OUT=tmp/invariants.tsv
JOBS=6
FILTER=""
TIMEOUT=900
SEEDS=""
NO_COMPETE=""

while [ $# -gt 0 ]; do
    case "$1" in
        --out) OUT="$2"; shift 2 ;;
        --jobs) JOBS="$2"; shift 2 ;;
        --filter) FILTER="$2"; shift 2 ;;
```
</details>

        --filter) FILTER="$2"; shift 2 ;;
        --timeout) TIMEOUT="$2"; shift 2 ;;
        --seeds) SEEDS="$2"; shift 2 ;;
        --no-compete) NO_COMPETE=1; shift ;;
        *) echo "unknown arg: $1" >&2; exit 2 ;;
    esac
done

mkdir -p "$(dirname "$OUT")"
LOG="$OUT.log"
: > "$LOG"

command -v gtimeout >/dev/null && TO=gtimeout || TO=timeout

run_one() {
    local model="$1" step="$2"
    local stl="tests/$model"
    local name="${model}"
    local env_args=("BREPPER_INVARIANTS=1")
    [ -n "$SEEDS" ] && env_args+=("BREPPER_INV_SEEDS=$SEEDS")
    [ -n "$NO_COMPETE" ] && env_args+=("BREPPER_INV_NO_COMPETE=1")
    local out err
    err=$(mktemp)
    out=$(env "${env_args[@]}" $TO "$TIMEOUT" "$BREPPER" "$stl" --compare "$step" \
              --stage 2.2 --invariant-report -v 2>"$err" )
    local rc=$?
    {
        echo "=== $name (rc=$rc)"
        cat "$err"
    } >> "$LOG"
    rm -f "$err"
    # Rewrite the model column to the full corpus-relative stem so rows from
    # two directories with the same basename stay distinguishable.
    echo "$out" | awk -v m="${model%.stl}" -F'\t' 'BEGIN{OFS="\t"} /^(INV|SEEDRULE)\t/ {$2=m; print}'
}

# sweep.sh --list pads the model column with spaces, and some model paths
# contain single spaces ("OrcaCube v2.stl"), so the separator is 2+ spaces.
pairs=$(./tools/sweep.sh --list 2>/dev/null \
        | sed -E 's/[[:space:]][[:space:]]+/\t/' \
        | awk -F'\t' 'NF==2 && $2 != ""')
[ -n "$FILTER" ] && pairs=$(echo "$pairs" | grep -E "$FILTER")

n=$(echo "$pairs" | grep -c .)
echo "invariants: $n model(s), jobs=$JOBS, timeout=${TIMEOUT}s" >&2

PAIRFILE=$(mktemp)
echo "$pairs" > "$PAIRFILE"

# bash 3.2 (macOS) has no \`wait -n\`, so hold the job count with a poll.
while IFS=$'\t' read -r model step; do
    [ -z "$model" ] && continue
    [ -z "$step" ] && continue
    while [ "$(jobs -rp | wc -l | tr -d ' ')" -ge "$JOBS" ]; do sleep 0.3; done
    run_one "$model" "$step" >> "$OUT" &
done < "$PAIRFILE"
wait
rm -f "$PAIRFILE"

echo "wrote $OUT ($(grep -c '^INV' "$OUT") reference-face rows); stderr in $LOG" >&2
```

**After:**
```
/// **Family K — the kinematic rank ladder**, measured on truth.
///
/// SURFACE_FITTING.md, "Nested fitting: a rank ladder instead of a type ladder",
/// proposes reading the surface type from the *null-space dimension* of the
/// tangent-similarity-field system rather than from a residual, so that a patch
/// with too little support reads as a simpler surface instead of a wrong one.
/// This asks the corpus four questions about that proposal, per reference face,
/// over a BFS patch grown from several starts and sampled at a ladder of support
/// sizes:
///
/// 1. **Is the signal there to be read?** The residual of the reference's *own*
///    tangent generators against the measured rows — the family-F question asked
///    of rank rather than of distance. Reported at both evaluation points, which
///    is how the centroid-vs-circumcenter choice gets settled by measurement
///    rather than by the derivation in [`super::kinematic::Eval`].
/// 2. **At what support does the read become correct**, and does it pass through
///    a *wrong* type on the way, or only through simpler ones? Only the second
///    is acceptable — the whole claim is that under-determination degrades to
///    simplicity.
/// 3. **How believable is the reading?** The ratio gap that produced the
///    dimension. A gap of 1e15 is a different object from a gap of 3.
/// 4. **Does straddling govern here too?** The fraction of the support whose
///    facet normals are not ruling-exact, alongside the reading.
///
/// Growth is confined to the reference face's own facets: this measures the
/// signal in the absence of junction contamination, which is the precondition
/// for the contaminated question being worth asking.
fn kinematic_probe(
    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
    faces: &[usize],
    surf: &RefSurface,
    model: &str,
    ref_idx: usize,
) {
    use super::kinematic::{
        dilation_about, rotation_about, translation_along, Eval, KinematicType, System,
    };

    const SIZES: [usize; 10] = [3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 12, 16, 24, 32, 48];
    const STARTS: usize = 6;

    // The type the ladder should converge on, and the reference's own tangent
    // generators — the ones that must lie in the null space if the read is to
    // have any chance.
    let (want, generators): (KinematicType, Vec<Box<dyn Fn(super::kinematic::Frame) -> _>>) =
        match *surf {
            RefSurface::Plane { point, normal } => {
                let (e1, e2) = perpendicular_basis(&unit(normal));
                (
                    KinematicType::Plane,
                    vec![
                        Box::new(move |f| translation_along(e1, f)),
                        Box::new(move |f| translation_along(e2, f)),
                        Box::new(move |f| rotation_about(point, normal, f)),
                    ],
                )
            }
            RefSurface::Sphere { centre, .. } => (
                KinematicType::Sphere,
                vec![
                    Box::new(move |f| rotation_about(centre, [1.0, 0.0, 0.0], f)),
                    Box::new(move |f| rotation_about(centre, [0.0, 1.0, 0.0], f)),
                    Box::new(move |f| rotation_about(centre, [0.0, 0.0, 1.0], f)),
                ],
            ),
            RefSurface::Cylinder {
                axis_pos, axis_dir, ..
            } => (
                KinematicType::Cylinder,
                vec![
                    Box::new(move |f| rotation_about(axis_pos, axis_dir, f)),
                    Box::new(move |f| translation_along(axis_dir, f)),
                ],
            ),
            RefSurface::Cone {
                apex, axis_dir, ..
            } => (
                KinematicType::Cone,
                vec![
                    Box::new(move |f| rotation_about(apex, axis_dir, f)),
                    Box::new(move |f| dilation_about(apex, f)),
                ],
            ),
            RefSurface::Torus {
                centre, axis_dir, ..
            } => (
                KinematicType::Revolution,
                vec![Box::new(move |f| rotation_about(centre, axis_dir, f))],
            ),
            RefSurface::Other { .. } => return,
        };

    // Straddling is defined against an axis, so it is only meaningful on the
    // surfaces that have one.
    let axis = surf.axis();

    let member: HashSet<usize> = faces.iter().copied().collect();
    let stride = (faces.len() / STARTS).max(1);
    for &start in faces.iter().step_by(stride).take(STARTS) {
        let mut patch = vec![start];
        let mut seen: HashSet<usize> = HashSet::from([start]);
        let mut frontier = vec![start];
        let mut next = 0usize;
        loop {
            while next < SIZES.len() && patch.len() >= SIZES[next] {
                let target = SIZES[next];
                next += 1;
                let sub = &patch[..target];

                let Some(sys) = System::build(sub, mesh, Eval::Centroid) else {
                    continue;
                };
                let sp = sys.spectrum();
                let (got, dim, gap) = sp.read();

                // Q1: is the truth in the measured null space, at each
                // evaluation point?
                let gen_cen = generators
                    .iter()
                    .map(|g| sys.generator_residual(g(sys.frame())))
                    .fold(0.0_f64, f64::max);
                let gen_cir = match System::build(sub, mesh, Eval::Circumcenter) {
                    Some(c) => generators
                        .iter()
                        .map(|g| c.generator_residual(g(c.frame())))
                        .fold(0.0_f64, f64::max),
                    None => f64::NAN,
                };

                // Q4: how much of this support has a normal that is not
                // ruling-exact?
                let straddle = match axis {
                    Some((pos, dir)) => {
                        let n = sub
                            .iter()
                            .filter(|&&fi| {
                                super::initialize::min_ruling_gap_rad(mesh, fi, pos, dir)
                                    .is_none_or(|g| g >= super::initialize::RULING_SAME_RAD)
                            })
                            .count();
                        n as f64 / sub.len() as f64
                    }
                    None => f64::NAN,
                };

                let top = sp.values[6].max(f64::MIN_POSITIVE);
                let spectrum: Vec<String> = sp
                    .values
                    .iter()
                    .map(|v| format!("{:.3e}", v / top))
                    .collect();
                println!(
                    "KINEMATIC\t{model}\t{ref_idx}\t{kind}\t{start}\t{target}\t{dim}\t{read}\t\
                     {ok}\t{gap:.3e}\t{straddle:.4}\t{gen_cen:.3e}\t{gen_cir:.3e}\t{spec}",
                    kind = surf.type_name(),
                    read = got.name(),
                    ok = u8::from(got == want),
                    spec = spectrum.join("\t"),
                );
            }
            if next >= SIZES.len() {
                break;
            }
            let mut ring = Vec::new();
            for &fi in &frontier {
                let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
                for &ni in &mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[..vc] {
                    if ni < 0 {
                        continue;
                    }
                    let nj = ni as usize;
                    if member.contains(&nj) && seen.insert(nj) {
                        patch.push(nj);
                        ring.push(nj);
                    }
                }
            }
            if ring.is_empty() {
                break;
            }
            frontier = ring;
        }
    }
}

/// clause, and fit each chain's closed-form axis against ground truth.

<!-- msg_011CdnBY8Vq2WgBrMa6zoeYb -->

> I'm spotting a critical issue with the heuristic: the null dimension decreases monotonically with support size regardless of the actual type, only matching the correct rank when support aligns with what the type needs, then it overshoots into freeform territory. The culprit is the "widest ratio gap" approach—the spectrum's small eigenvalues aren't truly zero in float32 precision.
> 
> Looking at the actual residuals, the sphere's generators at the centroid are around 1e-2 relative (catastrophically wrong), but at the circumcenter they're 1e-7 (correct). Since the probe reads the spectrum from the centroid, the sphere's "null" eigenvalues get polluted to roughly 1e-4 relative, which sits well above the numerical floor and causes the widest gap to collapse, misreading the dimension as zero. The fix is to read the spectrum at the right evaluation point, but that requires knowing which point is appropriate beforehand.
> 
> The cone's residuals are around 1e-3 at both centroid and circumcenter, which I think traces back to straddling facets — their normals can be off by ~1.7e-2 radians, consistent with that magnitude of error. The claim that a facet plane contains the apex only holds under specific geometric conditions that I'm still working through.
> 
> Now I'm seeing a clearer pattern across the test cases: the choice of evaluation point (circumcenter vs centroid) creates massive differences — five orders of magnitude for the sphere — and this is real measured data, not just theory. The "widest ratio gap" heuristic for determining rank is breaking down because the spectrum's null eigenvalues are buried in the data's noise floor rather than being truly zero.
> 
> As I trace through the support levels, the mechanism becomes obvious: each new row added to the matrix is generically independent, so with k rows the rank is at most k, meaning exactly 6-k eigenvalues are zero. This explains why the widest gap reading simply reports 7 minus the number of rows until you hit seven rows or more, at which point there are no exact zeros left and the heuristic collapses into whatever the noise tells it.
> 
> The coincidences that made my rule seem to work—sphere correct at support 4, cone correct at support 5—are just artifacts of this trivial rank bound: 7-4=3 and 7-5=2 happen to match the true dimensions, but that's not a real measurement, it's luck. The probe has exposed this flaw, which is actually valuable feedback. The real signal must come from where the eigenvalues transition from being structurally zero to being driven by data.
> 
> Now I need to look at the full eigenvalue spectra at larger support sizes to see where that gap actually appears. For a true cone, I'd expect to see noise-level eigenvalues at indices 0 and 1, then signal-level eigenvalues from 2 onward, with a clear jump between them. Let me pull the complete spectra and examine them directly.

> The plane geometry checks out perfectly with four exact zeros and three signal values, confirming it reads correctly at every support point. For the sphere at the centroid, I'm seeing the spectrum with those three smallest eigenvalues—1.9e-5, 4.8e-4, and 6.8e-4—appearing exactly where the null eigenvalues should be at the start of the sequence.
> 
> The real problem is that the gap between the third and fourth eigenvalues is only about 10×, which isn't a reliable separation on noisy data. My code has a hard-coded 1e-12 threshold that's forcing a dimension-0 read whenever the smallest eigenvalue exceeds that relative tolerance, which is exactly the kind of arbitrary threshold I wanted to avoid. This guard is throwing away legitimate readings on real noisy data. The centroid evaluation shows the signal eigenvalues starting at 6.5e-3, only a factor of 10 away from the last null eigenvalue—that's too close to be trustworthy. The circumcenter approach might give better separation with a gen_x value around 2.75.
> 
> For the cone, the centroid spectrum shows a ~39× gap between the second and third eigenvalues, which is better than the sphere's 10× but still not clean enough.  The culprit is facet straddling — about 27–50% of facets cross the true surface with normals off by ~1°, which inflates the "null" eigenvalues to around 3e-4, matching what I'm seeing. So on real meshes the rank gap is only 10–40×, not the 1e12 from perfect synthetic data, and that gap is entirely governed by facet-normal error, exactly as predicted. Before I can assess whether the reading rule works at different support sizes, I need to fix two things: report the spectrum at both evaluation points since the type-dependence is real and measured, and fix the `null_dim_by_widest_gap` function.
> 
> The hard 1e-12 threshold is a trap I warned myself about — I should drop it and instead report the widest ratio gap across all positions honestly. But even then, the rank-bound artifact (exactly `7 - rows` zeros) dominates at small support, so the principled rule is to only read a dimension when rows ≥ 7 and report Underdetermined below that, since fewer than 7 rows makes trivial zeros indistinguishable from real ones. Let me apply these fixes and remeasure — the probe revealed that my reading rule was actually measuring `7 - rows`, which is a genuine defect I need to correct.
> 
> Now I'm clarifying the support cost: you genuinely need at least 7 facets before the spectrum tells you anything meaningful, because below that the rank bound produces spurious zeros. This is a stricter requirement than my documentation table suggested (which listed 3/4/5/6 rows as viable), since that table was about ideal rank under perfect conditions, whereas readable rank needs 7+ rows to distinguish a true null space from row starvation. With 6 rows on a torus I can achieve rank 6 with exactly one zero, but I can't tell if that zero comes from the torus structure or just from having only 6 rows — and on a cylinder with 6 rows I'd get rank 5 (two zeros) versus the trivial one, so the distinction starts to emerge, but it's still ambiguous.
> 
> The rule I'm settling on is: **if the observed null dimension d satisfies d > 7 - k (where k is the number of rows), that's real structure; if d == 7 - k, it's row starvation and I should report Underdetermined.** This gives me a clean, threshold-free criterion. Let me work through the cases: with 3 rows the trivial null dimension is 4, and a plane also gives 4, so that's uninformative — I need 4 rows to confirm a plane (trivial 3, plane 4 > 3 ✓). With 4 rows, trivial is 3 and a plane gives 4, which is informative, but a sphere gives 3 = trivial, so that's not distinguishable. With 5 rows I can start to separate these cases more reliably.
> 
> Continuing through the rest: 5 rows has trivial 2, sphere 3 > 2 ✓, but cylinder/cone 2 = trivial ✗. With 6 rows, trivial is 1, cylinder/cone 2 > 1 ✓, but torus 1 = trivial ✗. With 7 rows, trivial is 0, torus 1 > 0 ✓, but freeform 0 = trivial ✗ (which is correct — I can never confirm freeform, only fail to confirm anything else). So the minimum support to confirm each type is: plane 4, sphere 5, cylinder/cone 6, torus 7 — exactly one more than the rank. That's the honest support cost and it's a clean derivation. Now I need to update the documentation table and fix the code to implement this rule, then re-measure the results.

    /// The null dimension implied by the widest *ratio* gap in the spectrum.
    ///
    /// Reported, and used to order candidate interpretations. **Not a decision.**

    ///

    /// how believable the reading is.
    pub(crate) fn null_dim_by_widest_gap(&self) -> (usize, f64) {
        let top = self.values[6];
        if !(top > 0.0) {
            return (7, f64::INFINITY);

        best
    }

    /// The null dimension **that row starvation alone does not already explain**,
    /// with the ratio gap that produced it.
    ///
    /// The subtlety this exists for, found by measurement rather than by
    /// derivation: with `k` rows the Gram matrix has rank at most `k`, so
    /// `7 − k` of its eigenvalues are exactly zero **for a reason that has
    /// nothing to do with the surface**. A naive widest-gap read therefore just
    /// reports `7 − rows` and looks impressively confident doing it — measured
    /// on `simple_sphere` and `simple_cone`, it walked 4, 3, 2, 1, 0 as the
    /// support went 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, matching the true type exactly once each, by
    /// coincidence, at the size where `7 − k` happened to equal it.
    ///
    /// So only positions **above** the trivial floor are considered. The
    /// consequence is a support cost of one row more than the type's rank:
    ///
    /// | type | rank | rows to *confirm* it |
    /// |---|---|---|
    /// | plane | 3 | 4 |
    /// | sphere | 4 | 5 |
    /// | cylinder, cone | 5 | 6 |
    /// | torus / revolution | 6 | 7 |
    ///
    /// with freeform never confirmable — only ever "nothing simpler was
    /// confirmed", which is the correct epistemic status for it.
    ///
    /// **The returned ratio is the whole point and must not be thresholded
    /// here.** On exact synthetic data it is ~1e13; on a real mesh it is set by
    /// facet-normal error and can be as low as ~10. Report it, order candidates
    /// by it, and let validation decide — see
    /// `initialize::MIN_NULLSPACE_SEPARATION` for the measurement that killed
    /// the idea of a cut.
    pub(crate) fn null_dim(&self) -> (usize, f64) {
        let top = self.values[6];
        if !(top > 0.0) {
            return (7, f64::INFINITY);
        }
        let trivial = 7usize.saturating_sub(self.rows);
        if trivial >= 6 {
            return (trivial, 0.0);
        }
        // Floor-clamp so an exact zero gives a large finite ratio rather than
        // an infinity that cannot be compared.
        let floor = top * 1.0e-18;
        let mut best = (trivial + 1, 0.0_f64);
        for k in (trivial + 1)..7 {
            let lo = self.values[k - 1].max(floor);
            let hi = self.values[k].max(floor);
            let ratio = hi / lo;
            if ratio > best.1 {
                best = (k, ratio);
            }
        }
        best
    }

<!-- msg_011CdnBkDnQby9EsQK5wEFeg -->

> I'm working through why the `null_dim` function returned 4 for a cylinder with 5 rows. The eigenvalue spectrum has zeros at the start, then 5 nonzero values, and my search begins at position 3 to find the largest gap between consecutive eigenvalues.
> 
> The issue is that my guard condition `dim + rows <= 7` correctly identifies that dim=4 is informative (since 9 > 7), but the classification still returns Plane when it should return Cylinder. The real problem is that my search is forced to skip the true dimensionality (which is 2, below the trivial floor of 3) and pick the next largest gap instead. I need to revise the logic to search across all positions and detect when the widest gap occurs at or below the trivial threshold—that's the signal that something went wrong with the search.
> 
> Let me verify this with concrete examples: a 5-row cylinder has spectrum [0, 0, λ₂...λ₆] with trivial floor 2, and the widest gap is at position 2 (the jump from clamped 0 to λ₂), so k=2 ≤ trivial means Underdetermined. For 6 rows, the trivial floor is 1, the widest gap is still at position 2, but now k=2 > trivial=1, so it correctly classifies as Cylinder with dim 2. With 7+ rows, the trivial floor is 0, and the widest gap at position 2 gives dim 2 as Cylinder. For 4 rows, the spectrum has three zeros, and the logic continues similarly.
> 
> Now checking planes and spheres: a 3-row plane has trivial floor 4 with widest gap at k=4, so k=4 ≤ trivial means Underdetermined (correct, since 3 rows can't confirm a plane). A 4-row plane has trivial floor 3 with widest gap at k=4, so k=4 > trivial=3 correctly identifies it as Plane with dim 4. For spheres, a 4-row sphere has trivial floor 3 with gap at k=3, giving Underdetermined, while a 5-row sphere has trivial floor 2 with gap at k=3, correctly identifying it as Sphere with dim 3. The pattern holds for higher-dimensional shapes like tori as well.

            let lo = self.values[k - 1].max(floor);
            let hi = self.values[k].max(floor);
            let ratio = hi / lo;
            if ratio > best.1 {
                best = (k, ratio);
            }
        }
        best
    }
```

        // Floor-clamp so an exact zero gives a large finite ratio rather than
        // an infinity that cannot be compared.
        let floor = top * 1.0e-18;
        let mut best = (0usize, 0.0_f64);
        for k in 1..7 {
            let lo = self.values[k - 1].max(floor);
            let hi = self.values[k].max(floor);
            let ratio = hi / lo;
            if ratio > best.1 {
                best = (k, ratio);
            }
        }
        // The gap is read over the whole spectrum and *then* compared against
        // the trivial floor, rather than searched for above it: restricting the
        // search would force a wrong answer whenever the true dimension sits at
